# Conserved co-abundance modules: kidney and lung

## Module 0

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (29)**: SLC3A2, ACSL3, APMAP, ATL3, CANX, ALCAM, CKAP4, FAM3C, GALNT2, GPD2, IKBIP, ITGA3, ITGAV, ITGB1, LNPEP, POR, NCSTN, DDOST, PMS1, PON2, PVR, RAB18, RAB5B, RAB5C, RAB8A, RHOG, RTN4, SEC22B, TMEM214

### Function

The strongest interpretation is an endoplasmic-reticulum and endomembrane trafficking module. Globally corrected cellular-component enrichment places many members in the ER membrane, the ER subcompartment, the nuclear-envelope–ER membrane network, organelle membranes, and the ER tubular network (GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum membrane; q_global = 2.99e-05), (GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum subcompartment; q_global = 2.99e-05), (GO:CC nuclear outer membrane-endoplasmic reticulum membrane network; q_global = 3.55e-05), (GO:CC organelle membrane; q_global = 0.000136), (GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum; q_global = 0.0162), (GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum tubular network; q_global = 0.038). The module also contains a coherent vesicle and endosomal component, supported by enrichment for cytoplasmic-vesicle, vesicle, secretory-granule, and plasma-membrane compartments (GO:CC secretory granule membrane; q_global = 0.000805), (GO:CC cytoplasmic vesicle membrane; q_global = 0.00116), (GO:CC vesicle membrane; q_global = 0.00146), (GO:CC plasma membrane; q_global = 0.0234). Functionally, the genes represent ER protein folding and glycosylation, ER-to-Golgi and endosomal trafficking, membrane recycling, cell adhesion, and phagocytosis. The globally corrected CAM-interaction and phagocytosis terms (KEGG Cell adhesion molecule (CAM) interaction - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 0.0148), (KEGG Phagocytosis - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 0.0336) support a membrane-interface and uptake function rather than a narrowly specialized biochemical pathway. The melanosome and pigment-granule terms (GO:CC melanosome; q_global = 0.00019), (GO:CC pigment granule; q_global = 0.00019) are best interpreted as evidence for conserved vesicular and organelle-membrane biology, not as evidence that the kidney-lung module is specifically a pigment module.

**Cell composition.** This is predominantly a membrane and intracellular-organelle module, not a plasma-protein module. HPA indicates low tissue specificity for most members, with only limited tissue-enhanced or tissue-enriched signals. The single-cell annotations are strongly cell-type enhanced, especially for neutrophils and related myeloid populations, while several genes are associated with trophoblast, epithelial, endothelial-related, or other specialized cell types. Only a minority are annotated as secreted, although ALCAM and PON2 are secreted to blood and FAM3C is secreted in other tissues. Therefore, the module is not best described as a bulk plasma or secreted-protein signature. However, the HPA evidence leaves open a contribution from shared blood or myeloid-cell content in kidney and lung samples, particularly because neutrophil and monocyte-related annotations recur. The detection of many proteins in both kidney and lung is consistent with a shared cellular membrane and trafficking program, but does not by itself distinguish intrinsic tissue-cell biology from shared cell-type composition.

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING supports several established relationships within the module. These include the integrin associations among ITGA3, ITGAV, and ITGB1, ALCAM with ITGB1, the ER relationships among CANX, DDOST, NCSTN, and SEC22B, the ER-shaping relationship between ATL3 and RTN4, the endosomal relationship between LNPEP and RAB8A, and the RAB5B-RAB5C relationship. These interactions provide a mechanistic scaffold linking cell adhesion, ER quality control, and vesicle trafficking. The STRING enrichment results (STRING Component Intrinsic component of membrane; within-query FDR = 7.81e-09) through (STRING Keyword Signal-anchor; within-query FDR = 0.0005) independently reinforce membrane, transmembrane, ER, organelle, and vesicle localization, but they remain supplementary rather than globally corrected module-level evidence. hu.MAP provides repeated complex-level support for an adhesion and cell-surface assembly containing ITGB1, ITGA3, ALCAM, and LNPEP (hu.MAP huMAP3_10503.1) through (hu.MAP huMAP3_09105.1) and (hu.MAP huMAP3_08689.1) through (hu.MAP huMAP3_11349.1). It also supports a RAB5B-RAB5C-RAB8A trafficking group (hu.MAP huMAP3_08941.1), (hu.MAP huMAP3_09217.1), (hu.MAP huMAP3_11094.1), (hu.MAP huMAP3_07239.1), (hu.MAP huMAP3_11860.1), as well as POR-RAB18 associations (hu.MAP huMAP3_08639.1), (hu.MAP huMAP3_05921.1), (hu.MAP huMAP3_10008.1). Thus, the module is not merely a list of unrelated membrane genes: several subsets correspond to known adhesion, trafficking, and ER-associated assemblies. At the same time, many module edges are not represented by high-confidence STRING interactions or hu.MAP complexes, so co-abundance should not be equated with direct physical binding.

### Novelty

The individual biology is not wholly new: integrin adhesion, ER chaperoning and glycosylation, RAB-mediated trafficking, and ER-network organization are established processes, and several relationships are already represented in STRING or hu.MAP. The potentially novel result is the conserved kidney-lung co-abundance combination that joins these processes in one cross-tissue module. The fact sheet does not establish that this exact module has been reported previously, and absence of an exact match in STRING or hu.MAP would not prove novelty because those resources primarily describe physical interactions or complexes rather than tissue-pair co-abundance modules. Accordingly, the appropriate conclusion is that this is a plausible novel systems-level module configuration, while its component mechanisms are largely established. A formal novelty claim would require comparison with published kidney-lung co-expression or co-abundance modules and independent replication in additional datasets.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module represents a shared structural and functional program in kidney and lung cells that maintains ER architecture and protein quality control, moves newly synthesized or recycled membrane proteins through ER, Golgi, and endosomal compartments, and couples this trafficking to integrin-mediated adhesion and uptake. In this model, CANX, DDOST, NCSTN, ATL3, RTN4, and SEC22B support ER organization, glycoprotein maturation, and vesicle exchange; RAB5B, RAB5C, RAB8A, RAB18, and LNPEP support endosomal or vesicular routing; and ITGA3, ITGAV, ITGB1, and ALCAM connect the trafficking system to cell adhesion and membrane signaling. The shared signal could reflect a conserved epithelial or stromal membrane program, a common myeloid-cell contribution, or both. This hypothesis should be tested by cell-resolved expression analysis, microscopy of ER and endosomal compartments, and perturbation of selected adhesion or trafficking genes.

### Important genes

#### ITGB1

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): IKBIP (0.83), CANX (0.82), ITGAV (0.82), NCSTN (0.81), PON2 (0.81), ITGA3 (0.81), PVR (0.81); STRING partners: ALCAM (0.824), ITGA3 (0.999), ITGAV (0.999)
- **HPA**: Low tissue specificity; Cell type enhanced; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Plasma membrane, Focal adhesion sites; IHC detected in kidney
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: huMAP3_10503.1, huMAP3_10980.1, huMAP3_09336.1, huMAP3_06815.1
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: integrin subunit beta 1. Integrins are heterodimeric proteins made up of alpha and beta subunits. At least 18 alpha and 8 beta subunits have been described in mammals. Integrin family members are membrane receptors involved in cell adhesion and recognition in a variety of processes including embryogenesis, hemostasis, tissue repair, immune response and metastatic diffusion of tumor cells. This gene encodes a beta subunit.…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: Crohn disease (0.560); viral infectious disease (0.527); inflammation (0.371); Anisometropia (0.331); type 1 diabetes mellitus (0.323)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 12192
- **Why important**: ITGB1 is the strongest network hub in the supplied module and connects to IKBIP, CANX, ITGAV, NCSTN, PON2, ITGA3, and PVR. It is present in many globally corrected membrane, organelle, phagocytosis, and CAM terms (GO:CC organelle membrane; q_global = 0.000136), (GO:CC melanosome; q_global = 0.00019), (GO:CC cytoplasmic vesicle membrane; q_global = 0.00116), (GO:CC membrane; q_global = 0.00398), (KEGG Cell adhesion molecule (CAM) interaction - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 0.0148), (KEGG Phagocytosis - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 0.0336). Its established function as an integrin beta subunit provides a direct link to cell adhesion, matrix recognition, immune response, tissue repair, angiogenesis, and phagocytosis. The ITGA3-ITGB1 and ITGAV-ITGB1 STRING relationships and several hu.MAP adhesion complexes further support its central structural role. Its extensive disease and literature associations make it the best-established translational anchor of the module.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports ITGB1 as a central adhesion and membrane-signaling component that helps organize the module. It is plausible, but not proven, that ITGB1-mediated adhesion coordinates the ER and vesicle program in the shared kidney-lung state.

- **Function (MyGene.info)**: nicastrin. This gene encodes a type I transmembrane glycoprotein that is an integral component of the multimeric gamma-secretase complex. The encoded protein cleaves integral membrane proteins, including Notch receptors and beta-amyloid precursor protein, and may be a stabilizing cofactor required for gamma-secretase complex assembly. The cleavage of beta-amyloid precursor protein yields amyloid beta peptide…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: acne inversa, familial, 1 (0.724); neurodegenerative disease (0.525); desmoid tumor (0.503); Alzheimer disease (0.406); hidradenitis suppurativa (0.389)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 2357
- **Why important**: NCSTN has high network centrality and connects ER-associated genes including CANX, CKAP4, RAB18, FAM3C, ACSL3, and ITGB1. It appears across a broad set of globally corrected ER, organelle-membrane, vesicle, secretory-granule, and plasma-membrane terms (GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum membrane; q_global = 2.99e-05) through (KEGG Phagocytosis - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 0.0336). Its established role as a stabilizing component of the gamma-secretase complex links the module to regulated cleavage of integral membrane proteins, including Notch receptors. The CANX-NCSTN STRING relationship and its kidney and lung protein detection strengthen the case that it is an intrinsic component rather than an isolated annotation.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports NCSTN as an ER-linked membrane-protein processing component connecting quality control with signaling-receptor proteolysis. Its precise contribution to the conserved kidney-lung co-abundance pattern remains a hypothesis.

It may also play a central role in the quality control of protein folding by retaining incorrectly folded protein subuni…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: neurodegenerative disease (0.462); COVID-19 (0.377); Orthomyxoviridae infectious disease (0.372); dengue disease (0.370); severe acute respiratory syndrome (0.370)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 2520
- **Why important**: CANX is a highly connected ER node linked to NCSTN, POR, ITGB1, PMS1, and ACSL3. Its established chaperone function in folding newly synthesized N-linked glycoproteins and retaining misfolded proteins provides a direct mechanistic explanation for the strong ER signal. STRING supports CANX relationships with DDOST, NCSTN, and SEC22B, integrating folding, glycosylation, and vesicle transport.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports CANX as a core ER quality-control component of the module. A possible but unproven interpretation is that coordinated CANX abundance marks increased membrane-protein biosynthesis or stress-adaptive trafficking in both tissues.

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): ATL3 (0.86), RAB5C (0.83), RAB18 (0.82), RAB5B (0.81), DDOST (0.81); STRING partners: CANX (0.703)

It seems to complex with SNARE and it is thought to play a role in the ER-Golgi protein trafficking. This protein has strong similarity to Mus musculus and Cricetulus griseus proteins.[provided by RefSeq, Sep 2009].
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: inflammatory bowel disease (0.285); luminal A breast carcinoma (0.271); colitis (0.238); enteritis (0.238); Crohn disease (0.233)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 1006
- **Why important**: SEC22B occupies a bridge position between ER and vesicle-related parts of the module, connecting ATL3, RAB5C, RAB18, RAB5B, and DDOST. It is included in globally corrected ER, organelle-membrane, vesicle, melanosome, and phagocytosis terms (GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum membrane; q_global = 2.99e-05) through (KEGG Phagocytosis - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 0.0336). Its established role as an ER-Golgi vesicle-trafficking protein and SNARE-associated vesicle-fusion factor makes it a strong mechanistic connector. The STRING relationship with CANX and the module's RAB associations support coordinated movement between ER and endosomal compartments.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports SEC22B as a vesicle-trafficking connector between ER organization and downstream membrane compartments. It is plausible, but not established, that SEC22B is a rate-limiting mediator of the shared kidney-lung program.

- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports ITGA3 as a tissue-architecture and cell-matrix adhesion component of the module. The hypothesis that ITGA3-ITGB1 couples extracellular matrix contact to ER and vesicle remodeling is consistent with the evidence but requires direct functional testing.

## Module 1

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (34)**: AATF, CSTF1, DDX21, EIF3L, EPS15L1, CLINT1, FAM120A, GON7, KNOP1, MYBBP1A, MED24, MYG1, NOP2, NUP214, NVL, PES1, PSME4, PYM1, CRYZL1, RBM10, RBM15, RBM22, RSL1D1, RRP1B, SEC16A, SEH1L, SNW1, CTTN, STRN, FARSB, TCOF1, UTP15, YLPM1, ZC3HAV1

### Function

The module is best interpreted as a conserved nuclear RNA-handling and ribosome-production program. The primary evidence is the global enrichment of RNA binding (GO:MF RNA binding; q_global = 8.34e-05), nucleolar localization (GO:CC nucleolus; q_global = 0.00226), nucleic-acid binding (GO:MF nucleic acid binding; q_global = 0.00915), ribosome biogenesis (GO:BP ribosome biogenesis; q_global = 0.0106), ribonucleoprotein-complex biogenesis (GO:BP ribonucleoprotein complex biogenesis; q_global = 0.0148), rRNA metabolism (GO:BP rRNA metabolic process; q_global = 0.0224), and RNA processing (GO:BP RNA processing; q_global = 0.048). The overlap among these terms indicates that the dominant biological theme is coordinated processing, modification, maturation, and handling of ribosomal and other nuclear RNAs, rather than a tissue-specific extracellular pathway. The network contains a coherent nucleolar core involving DDX21, NOP2, PES1, MYBBP1A, RRP1B, RSL1D1, UTP15, AATF, and related factors, together with an RNA-splicing and RNA-processing arm involving RBM15, RBM10, RBM22, SNW1, CSTF1, YLPM1, and FAM120A. The enrichment does not by itself establish that every member performs the same molecular task, but it supports a shared module-level program of RNA maturation and translational capacity.

**Cell composition.** This is not a plasma-protein or secreted-protein module: the HPA summary reports no secreted genes. Most members have low tissue specificity, consistent with broadly expressed, housekeeping-like nuclear functions. Protein detection is widespread in both kidney and lung for the annotated members, although some genes are not detected or lack data in one tissue. The module is therefore unlikely to represent a kidney- or lung-exclusive secretory program. HPA also shows low immune-cell specificity for most genes, arguing against a module composed primarily of blood cells. Nevertheless, a minority of members show enrichment in bone marrow, neutrophils, basophils, monocytes, or other specialized cell types, so blood or shared immune-cell admixture could contribute to some co-abundance signal. The broad expression pattern and nucleolar localization make a shared cellular maintenance program the more parsimonious interpretation.

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides direct high-confidence support for a nucleolar ribosome-biogenesis core. Known links include DDX21 with MYBBP1A, NOP2, PES1, RRP1B, and RSL1D1; MYBBP1A with NOP2, PES1, RSL1D1, and UTP15; NOP2 with PES1, RRP1B, RSL1D1, and NVL; PES1 with RRP1B and RSL1D1; and AATF or UTP15 with several of these nucleolar factors. STRING also supports the RBM22–SNW1 spliceosomal relationship and the NUP214–SEH1L nucleoporin relationship. These known interactions validate the biological coherence of the core, while the absence of STRING support for some module edges indicates that co-abundance is broader than currently catalogued physical or functional interaction evidence. hu.MAP independently identifies recurring pairs or small subsets involving SNW1 and RBM22, NUP214 and SEH1L, SEC16A with CLINT1 and EPS15L1, and ZC3HAV1 with FAM120A. The hu.MAP results therefore support recognizable spliceosomal, nuclear-pore, endocytic, and RNA-associated components, but they do not define one complete protein complex containing the full module.

### Novelty

The module should not be claimed as a wholly new molecular pathway. Its main functional themes, especially ribosome biogenesis, rRNA processing, RNA binding, and spliceosomal RNA processing, are established by the globally corrected annotations and by known STRING or hu.MAP relationships. The potentially novel aspect is the conserved co-abundance organization observed across kidney and lung, particularly the combination of a strongly connected nucleolar core with RNA-processing, nuclear-pore, and vesicle-trafficking members. However, the fact sheet does not provide an external replication cohort, perturbation experiment, or literature comparison specifically testing this cross-tissue module. Lack of STRING or hu.MAP coverage alone cannot establish novelty. The appropriate conclusion is that this is a biologically plausible and potentially useful cross-tissue co-abundance module, but its module-level novelty remains unproven.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects a shared cellular demand for coordinated RNA maturation and protein-production capacity in kidney and lung cells. A nucleolar core centered on DDX21, PES1, NOP2, MYBBP1A, RRP1B, RSL1D1, and UTP15 may coordinate rRNA processing and ribosome assembly, while RBM15, RBM10, RBM22, and SNW1 connect this program to pre-mRNA processing and nuclear RNA handling. SEC16A, CLINT1, EPS15L1, and FAM120A may represent coupling to vesicle or membrane-trafficking states rather than a second dominant pathway. The cross-tissue preservation could therefore arise from shared requirements for epithelial-cell maintenance, growth, stress adaptation, or translational output. This remains a hypothesis because co-abundance does not demonstrate physical binding, causal regulation, or directionality.

## Module 2

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (18)**: GOT2, ETFA, HMG20A, IDH3A, INTS4, LBR, MDN1, NDUFAF4, POLDIP2, PRDX3, CDC40, SMC5, SUCLG1, SUCLA2, TLE3, UTP11, XRCC1, ZMYND8

### Function

The strongest interpretation is a mitochondrial central-energy-metabolism module centered on the tricarboxylic acid cycle. The primary evidence is the globally corrected enrichment for the tricarboxylic acid cycle heteromeric enzyme complex (GO:CC tricarboxylic acid cycle heteromeric enzyme complex; q_global = 0.0118) and for succinate-CoA ligase activity and its complex (GO:MF succinate-CoA ligase (ADP-forming) activity; q_global = 0.0473), (GO:CC succinate-CoA ligase complex (ADP-forming); q_global = 0.0473). IDH3A, SUCLA2, and SUCLG1 provide the core biochemical signal. STRING independently supports the same interpretation through TCA-cycle and mitochondrial-matrix annotations (8 supplementary STRING terms; within-query FDR 0.0257–0.0495), although these are supplementary rather than globally corrected evidence. The remaining genes form weaker connected segments involving mitochondrial electron transfer, oxidative-stress protection, amino-acid metabolism, RNA processing, chromatin regulation, DNA repair, and nuclear-envelope biology. Thus, the module is not a uniform physical complex; it is best viewed as a co-abundance module whose principal signal is mitochondrial metabolic capacity with additional cellular-state components.

**Cell composition.** This is not a plasma-protein or secreted-protein module: the HPA summary reports no secreted genes, and protein detection is broadly observed in both kidney and lung for the annotated genes. Most genes have low immune-cell specificity, so the module is not primarily blood-cell enriched. However, HPA shows substantial cell-type enhancement across diverse populations, including hepatocytes, parietal cells, myonuclei, erythrocyte progenitors, and neutrophils, while tissue enrichment is most often seen in tongue and skeletal muscle, with additional liver, heart-muscle, and choroid-plexus signals. IDH3A has plasmacytoid-dendritic-cell enhancement, and TLE3 and LBR have neutrophil-related enrichment, but these observations do not establish that the kidney–lung module is caused by blood contamination. The pattern is more consistent with broadly distributed mitochondrial and cellular-state programs, potentially influenced by shared metabolically active cell types.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 3 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

| Term | q_global | genes |
|---|---|---|
| GO:CC tricarboxylic acid cycle heteromeric enzyme complex | 0.0118 | IDH3A, SUCLA2, SUCLG1 |
| GO:MF succinate-CoA ligase (ADP-forming) activity | 0.0473 | SUCLA2, SUCLG1 |
| GO:CC succinate-CoA ligase complex (ADP-forming) | 0.0473 | SUCLA2, SUCLG1 |

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides five high-confidence pairwise links within the module. The strongest direct relationship connects SUCLA2 and SUCLG1, consistent with the known heterodimeric succinate-CoA ligase; IDH3A is linked to both subunits and to GOT2, while ETFA is linked to SUCLG1. This supports a real metabolic core rather than a purely statistical grouping. STRING functional annotations also repeatedly recover the TCA cycle, the TCA enzyme complex, mitochondrial matrix localization, and transit-peptide annotations (8 supplementary STRING terms; within-query FDR 0.0257–0.0495). hu.MAP does not identify a module-wide complex. Its only repeated overlap is LBR with POLDIP2 across several complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_09155.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08786.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09852.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10150.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_13593.1), and each overlap contains only a small subset of the listed complex members. Therefore hu.MAP supports limited known complex context for LBR and POLDIP2, but not a single physical complex encompassing the module. The absence of a hu.MAP or STRING relationship for several gene pairs should not be interpreted as evidence that those co-abundance relationships are biologically false.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 5 of 153 gene pairs: ETFA–SUCLG1 (0.956); GOT2–IDH3A (0.889); IDH3A–SUCLA2 (0.972); IDH3A–SUCLG1 (0.902); SUCLA2–SUCLG1 (0.999)
**STRING functional enrichment (supplementary, within-query FDR)**: WikiPathways TCA cycle (aka Krebs or citric acid cycle) (FDR = 0.0257, 3 genes); Component Tricarboxylic acid cycle enzyme complex (FDR = 0.0271, 3 genes); COMPARTMENTS Mitochondrial matrix (FDR = 0.0296, 7 genes); COMPARTMENTS Tricarboxylic acid cycle enzyme complex (FDR = 0.0296, 3 genes); NetworkNeighborAL Citrate cycle (TCA cycle), and Lactate dehydrogenase activity (FDR = 0.0423, 4 genes); Keyword Tricarboxylic acid cycle (FDR = 0.046, 3 genes); Keyword Transit peptide (FDR = 0.0478, 7 genes); KEGG Citrate cycle (TCA cycle) (FDR = 0.0495, 3 genes)
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: huMAP3_09155.1 (LBR, POLDIP2; 2/23); huMAP3_08786.1 (LBR, POLDIP2; 2/24); huMAP3_09852.1 (LBR, POLDIP2; 2/28); huMAP3_10150.1 (LBR, POLDIP2; 2/38); huMAP3_13593.1 (LBR, POLDIP2; 2/42)

### Novelty

The biochemical core is not a new pathway discovery: the roles of IDH3A, SUCLA2, SUCLG1, GOT2, and ETFA in mitochondrial metabolism are established, and the corresponding relationships are represented in STRING. The potentially novel aspect is the cross-tissue co-abundance configuration linking this established metabolic core with nuclear, RNA-processing, DNA-repair, and chromatin-associated genes in kidney and lung. The available evidence does not demonstrate that this particular module has previously been reported, so it should be described as a potentially new tissue-shared network organization rather than a newly discovered molecular function. Absence from STRING or hu.MAP cannot establish novelty, because those resources primarily document known physical or functional relationships and may not capture tissue-specific co-abundance structure. Independent replication, comparison with published kidney and lung co-expression modules, and experimental validation would be required to claim a new finding.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects coordinated mitochondrial energetic and redox capacity in a shared metabolically active cellular population present in kidney and lung. In this model, IDH3A supplies TCA-cycle flux, SUCLA2 and SUCLG1 execute succinate-CoA ligase activity, GOT2 connects amino-acid and TCA metabolism, and ETFA supports electron transfer from fatty-acid oxidation. PRDX3 may buffer mitochondrial oxidative stress, while the nuclear and RNA-processing genes may represent a broader cellular maintenance or biosynthetic state that covaries with mitochondrial demand. This is a mechanistic hypothesis, not proof of direct regulation or physical assembly. The data support co-abundance and a metabolic core, but they do not establish causal direction, cell-type origin, or direct interaction among all module members.

Defects in electron-transfer-flavoprotein have been implicated in type II glutaricaciduria in which multiple acyl-CoA dehydrogenase deficiencies result in l…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: multiple acyl-CoA dehydrogenase deficiency (0.834); glutaric aciduria (0.756); hereditary disease (0.469); multiple acyl-CoA dehydrogenase deficiency, mild type (0.370); multiple acyl-CoA dehydrogenase deficiency, severe neonatal type (0.370)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 1433
- **Why important**: ETFA has high weighted connectivity within the module and connects to the metabolic core through SUCLA2, PRDX3, GOT2, SUCLG1, and IDH3A. STRING directly links it to SUCLG1, while supplementary mitochondrial-matrix and transit-peptide annotations include ETFA (STRING COMPARTMENTS Mitochondrial matrix; within-query FDR = 0.0296), (STRING Keyword Transit peptide; within-query FDR = 0.0478). Its established function is electron transfer from multiple acyl-CoA dehydrogenases toward the respiratory chain during fatty-acid beta-oxidation. It has a large literature base and strong disease associations with multiple acyl-CoA dehydrogenase deficiency and glutaric aciduria.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports ETFA as a connector between fatty-acid oxidation and the module's TCA-associated mitochondrial program. Its position suggests coordinated energy metabolism, but the module does not prove direct functional coupling between ETFA and the TCA enzymes in these tissues.

Glutamic-oxaloacetic transaminase is a pyridoxal phosphate-dependent enzyme which exists in cytoplasmic and inner-membrane mitochondrial forms, GOT1 and GOT2, respectively. GOT plays a role in amino acid metabolism and the urea and tricarboxylic acid cycles. The two enzymes are homodimeric and show close homology. Two transcript variants encoding different isoforms have been found for this gene. […
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: genetic developmental and epileptic encephalopathy (0.618); developmental and epileptic encephalopathy (0.393); Encephalopathy (0.370); neurodegenerative disease (0.356); lagophthalmos (0.298)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 2463
- **Why important**: GOT2 links amino-acid metabolism with the mitochondrial metabolic core through SUCLA2 and ETFA, and STRING connects it to IDH3A. Its HPA pattern is group enriched across several metabolically active tissues and cell types, including hepatocytes and urothelial cells, while its established function includes amino-acid, aspartate, glutamate, urea-cycle, and TCA-related metabolism. It also has a comparatively large literature base and documented neurological disease associations.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports GOT2 as a metabolic interface that could broaden the module beyond the canonical TCA-cycle core. Its tissue and cell-type pattern is compatible with a shared metabolic program, but the module does not establish whether GOT2 is a driver, a consequence, or simply a correlated marker of cellular metabolic state.

## Module 3

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (37)**: ACIN1, AIMP1, BCLAF1, BOP1, CBX5, CHD1, CHD4, CHORDC1, CNOT1, DHX36, DHX9, POLD1, ADAR, HNRNPR, MATR3, NAA38, NBN, NOL6, NOL8, NOP56, NUP85, PUM1, CAD, RPL12, DIS3, PDCD11, SEC23IP, SUPT16H, SRSF7, TBL3, ALYREF, TP53BP1, UTP20, WDR12, WDR26, WDR3, ZFR

### Function

The module is best interpreted as a conserved nuclear RNA and ribosome-biogenesis program. The strongest primary evidence is enrichment for nucleic-acid binding and RNA binding (GO:MF nucleic acid binding; q_global = 3.43e-08)(GO:MF RNA binding; q_global = 7.23e-07), RNA processing (GO:BP RNA processing; q_global = 8.1e-05), the preribosome and 90S preribosome (GO:CC 90S preribosome; q_global = 4.33e-05)(GO:CC preribosome; q_global = 0.000116), rRNA processing (GO:BP rRNA processing; q_global = 0.000298), rRNA metabolism (GO:BP rRNA metabolic process; q_global = 0.000926), ribonucleoprotein-complex biogenesis (GO:BP ribonucleoprotein complex biogenesis; q_global = 0.00101), the small-subunit processome (GO:CC small-subunit processome; q_global = 0.00146), and ribosome biogenesis (GO:BP ribosome biogenesis; q_global = 0.00328). The broad nuclear and nucleolar localization evidence (GO:CC nuclear lumen; q_global = 0.000186)(GO:CC nucleus; q_global = 0.0018)(GO:CC nucleoplasm; q_global = 0.00224)(GO:CC nucleolus; q_global = 0.0257) is consistent with this interpretation. The module also contains RNA surveillance, splicing, mRNA turnover, chromatin, DNA-repair, and transcription-related genes, indicating that it is not limited to a single preribosomal particle. The network separates into a strongly connected nucleolar preribosome group centered on BOP1, NOL6, NOP56, PDCD11, TBL3, UTP20, WDR3, and WDR12, plus RNA-binding and RNA-processing relationships involving DHX9, HNRNPR, ADAR, ALYREF, SRSF7, PUM1, MATR3, and ZFR. Thus, the most defensible function is coordinated production, processing, surveillance, and use of nuclear RNA, with a prominent ribosome-biogenesis component rather than a kidney- or lung-specific biochemical pathway.

**Cell composition.** This is not a plasma-protein or secreted-protein module: the HPA summary identifies only AIMP1 as secreted to blood. It is also not strongly blood-cell enriched in the HPA blood-cell classification, and nearly all genes have low immune-cell specificity. Most genes have low tissue specificity, with a minority showing tissue or cell-type enhancement, including bone marrow, skeletal muscle, choroid plexus, germ-cell populations, erythrocyte progenitors, and neutrophils. This pattern is compatible with a broadly expressed housekeeping-like nuclear program, especially because the proteins are detected in both kidney and lung for most tested genes. Some cell-type-enhanced annotations could reflect proliferative or differentiating cell populations, but the available HPA evidence does not support interpreting the shared module primarily as blood contamination or a common immune-cell infiltrate.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 25 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING independently supports the same biological interpretation through nuclear-lumen and nucleic-acid-binding enrichment (STRING COMPARTMENTS Nuclear lumen; within-query FDR = 1.17e-08)(STRING Function Nucleic acid binding; within-query FDR = 2.68e-08), RNA metabolism (STRING RCTM Metabolism of RNA; within-query FDR = 9.35e-07), rRNA processing (STRING RCTM Major pathway of rRNA processing in the nucleolus and cytosol; within-query FDR = 2.78e-06), and 90S preribosome annotations (STRING COMPARTMENTS 90S preribosome; within-query FDR = 3.57e-06)(STRING COMPARTMENTS Preribosome; within-query FDR = 7.49e-06)(STRING Component 90S preribosome; within-query FDR = 2.01e-05). The high-confidence STRING graph contains a coherent preribosome cluster linking BOP1, NOL6, NOP56, PDCD11, TBL3, UTP20, WDR3, and WDR12, as well as established RNA-processing relationships among ADAR, DHX9, ALYREF, HNRNPR, SRSF7, MATR3, and ZFR. hu.MAP provides orthogonal complex-level support for RNA-binding and messenger-RNA-processing assemblies: HNRNPR, MATR3, ADAR, PUM1, and ZFR recur together in (hu.MAP huMAP3_10625.1), while SRSF7, ALYREF, BCLAF1, and ACIN1 recur in (hu.MAP huMAP3_09943.1). Additional hu.MAP complexes reinforce the HNRNP and splicing-related group (hu.MAP huMAP3_09223.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_07581.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_07817.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_08817.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_09281.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_07115.1) and the SRSF7-BCLAF1-ACIN1 group (hu.MAP huMAP3_06097.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_10564.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_11073.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_06391.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_07267.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_09621.1). The STRING relationships are therefore largely established molecular associations, while the hu.MAP overlaps indicate that several module segments correspond to known multiprotein assemblies. The co-abundance module is consequently more consistent with coordinated activity of known nuclear systems than with a collection of unrelated genes.

### Novelty

The module should not be claimed as a wholly new molecular complex or as proof of a new kidney-lung mechanism. Its core functions and many relationships are already established by GO, STRING, and hu.MAP, particularly preribosome assembly, rRNA processing, RNA binding, RNA splicing, and RNA surveillance. Nevertheless, the conserved co-abundance pattern across kidney and lung may still be a useful systems-level finding: the specific tissue-pair module organization, the coupling of the nucleolar preribosome group to RNA-processing and chromatin-related genes, and its reproducibility across these tissues are not directly established by the database annotations alone. Novelty therefore lies, at most, in the observed cross-tissue co-abundance architecture and its biological integration, not in discovering previously unknown functions for the constituent genes. Absence of a gene or edge from STRING or hu.MAP is insufficient evidence for novelty.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module represents a shared cellular demand program in kidney and lung in which nucleolar preribosome assembly and rRNA maturation are coupled to nuclear RNA processing, export, decay, chromatin access, and DNA-damage control. In this model, the BOP1-NOL6-NOP56-PDCD11-TBL3-UTP20-WDR3-WDR12 group supports ribosome production, while DHX9, HNRNPR, ADAR, ALYREF, SRSF7, PUM1, MATR3, and ZFR coordinate processing, editing, transport, stability, and translation of RNA. SUPT16H, CHD1, CHD4, CBX5, NBN, and TP53BP1 could connect this RNA-production state to chromatin organization and genome surveillance. The shared kidney-lung signal may therefore reflect a common requirement for high-fidelity gene expression and cellular maintenance rather than a tissue-specific secretory or immune process. This remains a mechanistic hypothesis and requires perturbation, localization, and flux measurements to test.

#### WDR3

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): DHX36 (0.88), NOP56 (0.85), PUM1 (0.84), ADAR (0.83), TBL3 (0.83), NOL8 (0.83), ZFR (0.81); STRING partners: BOP1 (0.973), NOL6 (0.999), NOP56 (0.999), PDCD11 (0.997), TBL3 (0.999), UTP20 (0.999), WDR12 (0.954)

- **hu.MAP 3.0**: no complex with >= 2 module members
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: WD repeat domain 3. This gene encodes a nuclear protein containing 10 WD repeats. WD repeats are approximately 30- to 40-amino acid domains containing several conserved residues, which usually include a trp-asp at the C-terminal end. Proteins belonging to the WD repeat family are involved in a variety of cellular processes, including cell cycle progression, signal transduction, apoptosis, and gene regulation. [provid…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: presbycusis (0.335); respiratory tract infectious disorder (0.184); neurodegenerative disease (0.143); preeclampsia (0.120); alcohol drinking (0.098)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 280
- **Why important**: WDR3 has the strongest network position in the module and connects the nucleolar preribosome-associated genes with RNA-binding regulators including DHX36, PUM1, ADAR, NOL8, and ZFR. It is present across nearly all primary enrichment themes from nucleic-acid binding through ribosome biogenesis (24 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 3.43e-08–0.0337; see the table), is nucleolar by HPA, and is annotated for small-subunit rRNA maturation and ribosome biogenesis. Its STRING connectivity to the preribosome group is extensive, whereas it has no hu.MAP assignment in this fact sheet. The literature volume is substantial, and disease associations include presbycusis and respiratory tract infectious disorder.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports WDR3 as a central structural or organizational component of the module's nucleolar and RNA-processing axis. Its high co-abundance connectivity makes it an important module anchor. Whether WDR3 actively couples preribosome assembly to the broader RNA-binding subnetwork is a hypothesis rather than an established function here.

Its annotation identifies participation in pre-rRNA processing and ribosomal-subunit assembly, and it has the largest literature volume among the selected module genes. Its disease association with spinocerebellar ataxia type thirty-six further demonstrates clinical relevance.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence identifies NOP56 as a core nucleolar ribosome-biogenesis component and a strong biological representative of the module. The module does not establish that its disease-linked repeat expansion or other disease mechanisms operate in kidney or lung; that tissue-specific interpretation remains a hypothesis.

- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports DHX9 as a multifunctional RNA helicase linking RNA remodeling and processing to the nucleolar and transcriptional context of the module. Its position suggests a possible integrative role, but the co-abundance data do not prove that DHX9 mechanistically drives ribosome biogenesis or coordinates the tissue-shared program.

Its disease associations include neurodevelopmental disorders, intellectual disability, developmental delay, and seizure, with a substantial literature base.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports HNRNPR as a connector between the module's RNA-processing network and known heterogeneous nuclear ribonucleoprotein assemblies. Its cross-linking of submodules is consistent with a coordinating role, but causality and tissue-specific function are hypotheses.

#### ADAR

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): SRSF7 (0.84), WDR3 (0.83), NBN (0.80); STRING partners: DHX9 (0.922)
- **HPA**: Low tissue specificity; Cell type enhanced; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Nucleoli; IHC detected in kidney, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: huMAP3_10625.1
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: adenosine deaminase RNA specific. This gene encodes the enzyme responsible for RNA editing by site-specific deamination of adenosines. This enzyme destabilizes double-stranded RNA through conversion of adenosine to inosine. Mutations in this gene have been associated with dyschromatosis symmetrica hereditaria. Alternative splicing results in multiple transcript variants. [provided by RefSeq, Jul 2010].
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: Aicardi-Goutieres syndrome 6 (0.838); dyschromatosis symmetrica hereditaria (0.823); Aicardi-Goutieres syndrome (0.713); ADAR-related type 1 interferonopathy (0.568); hereditary disease (0.520)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 6966
- **Why important**: ADAR adds a distinct RNA-editing function to the module and connects by STRING to DHX9, while its co-abundance links include SRSF7, WDR3, and NBN. It is present in primary RNA-processing, nucleic-acid-binding, ribonucleoprotein, and gene-expression terms (GO:MF nucleic acid binding; q_global = 3.43e-08)(GO:MF RNA binding; q_global = 7.23e-07)(GO:BP RNA processing; q_global = 8.1e-05)(GO:BP ribonucleoprotein complex biogenesis; q_global = 0.00101)(GO:CC ribonucleoprotein complex; q_global = 0.00135)(GO:BP gene expression; q_global = 0.0097), is detected in both kidney and lung, and is annotated as the enzyme responsible for adenosine-to-inosine RNA editing. Its HPA cell-type enhancement in neutrophils is notable but does not establish an immune-cell origin for the entire module. ADAR has a substantial literature base and strong disease associations with Aicardi-Goutières syndrome and related interferonopathies.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports ADAR as the module's RNA-editing and innate-RNA-response component, embedded within a broader nuclear RNA-processing program. It is plausible that ADAR links RNA maturation to cellular stress or antiviral regulation, but that proposed coupling is not demonstrated by the module alone.

## Module 4

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (50)**: ACLY, AP1M1, APEX1, BAX, MTHFD1, CNPY2, COPB1, CPSF6, CACYBP, DNM1L, FKBP4, G6PD, GARS1, GMPS, HARS1, HDGF, HNRNPK, HPRT1, HSPH1, HSPA8, IDE, EIF5, IPO4, IQGAP1, PKM, NUP93, PCNA, PDCD6IP, PFKP, PSPC1, PTBP1, GART, PFAS, PAICS, CTPS1, RAN, RCC1, SF3A1, AARS1, MARS1, SARS1, TARS1, VARS1, TKT, TNPO1, TXNRD1, USP5, USO1, XPO1, XRCC6

### Function

The strongest interpretation is a conserved cellular biosynthetic and proteostasis module centered on purine and ribonucleotide metabolism, aminoacyl-tRNA charging, translation support, and associated vesicle, trafficking, and nuclear processes. Primary evidence comes from the globally corrected terms for ligase activity (GO:MF ligase activity; q_global = 1.26e-05), aminoacyl-tRNA ligase activity and tRNA aminoacylation (6 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 0.00139–0.00234; see the table), ribose phosphate and nucleotide metabolism (8 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 0.000188–0.00356; see the table), and multiple purine biosynthetic processes including GMP, IMP, AMP, and related monophosphate biosynthesis (13 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 0.00195–0.0485; see the table). The genes form a coherent enzymatic core: GART, PAICS, PFAS, GMPS, HPRT1, and MTHFD1 support purine production or salvage, while AARS1, GARS1, HARS1, MARS1, SARS1, TARS1, and VARS1 support aminoacyl-tRNA metabolism. Additional module structure connects glycolysis and pentose-phosphate metabolism, RNA processing, nuclear transport, DNA repair, chaperoning, and vesicle biology. The extracellular-vesicle and extracellular-region terms (7 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 2.02e-05–0.000391; see the table) should not be interpreted as evidence that the module is primarily secreted; most annotated proteins are intracellular and these terms can arise from vesicular release, annotation propagation, or proteins detected in extracellular vesicle preparations.

**Cell composition.** This is not predominantly a plasma-protein or secreted-protein module. HPA reports only a minority of secreted genes, while most genes have low tissue specificity and low immune-cell specificity. Protein detection is broadly present in both kidney and lung for nearly all genes with available data, supporting a shared cellular program rather than a kidney- or lung-exclusive protein. The module is therefore largely composed of broadly expressed housekeeping-type and biosynthetic proteins, including metabolic enzymes, translation factors, chaperones, nuclear transport proteins, and RNA-processing proteins. HPA does show cell-type-enhanced signals in reproductive, epithelial, progenitor, muscle, liver, and myeloid populations, and some enrichment for erythrocyte progenitors and myeloid cells, but the overall blood-cell specificity is low. A contribution from shared cell types or sample composition remains possible, especially for vesicle-associated signals, yet the coherent intracellular purine and translation machinery argues against a purely blood-contamination explanation.

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides established relationships within several recognizable subnetworks. The aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases form a densely connected group around AARS1, GARS1, HARS1, MARS1, SARS1, TARS1, and VARS1. The purine-biosynthesis group contains strong links among GART, GMPS, MTHFD1, PAICS, PFAS, CTPS1, and HPRT1. Glycolytic and pentose-phosphate relationships connect PKM, PFKP, G6PD, and TKT. Nuclear transport is represented by RAN, RCC1, XPO1, TNPO1, and IPO4, while RNA-processing and chaperone relationships include HNRNPK, PTBP1, CPSF6, HSPA8, and HSPH1. These known relationships support a biologically structured module rather than an arbitrary collection of co-abundant genes. hu.MAP independently supports complexes involving purine and central metabolism, aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases, chaperones, nuclear transport, and vesicle trafficking (hu.MAP huMAP3_14442.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_13695.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_12823.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_14154.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_03291.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_04783.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_11169.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_00676.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_11534.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_12466.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09597.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06494.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10115.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_02637.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_13688.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07331.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08641.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_13998.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09042.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07919.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_14982.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_11049.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_13774.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10000.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_14723.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_12581.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09592.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_12394.1). Particularly informative are complexes containing GART, GMPS, MTHFD1, and PKM (hu.MAP huMAP3_14442.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_13695.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_12823.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_14154.1), aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase pairs (hu.MAP huMAP3_04783.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_11534.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_02637.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_13688.1), RAN and RCC1 transport complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_09597.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06494.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10115.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_02637.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_13688.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07331.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08641.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10000.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_12581.1), chaperone complexes containing HSPA8 and HSPH1 (hu.MAP huMAP3_07919.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_11049.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_14723.1), and the vesicle-trafficking complex containing USO1 and AP1M1 (hu.MAP huMAP3_12394.1). The module therefore combines known molecular subsystems with cross-subsystem co-abundance; STRING and hu.MAP establish many component relationships but do not by themselves establish that the entire kidney-lung module is a single physical complex.

### Novelty

The underlying biochemical functions are not new: purine synthesis, aminoacyl-tRNA charging, glycolysis, nuclear transport, chaperoning, and vesicle trafficking are well-established processes, and many relationships are represented in STRING or hu.MAP. The potentially novel finding is the conserved co-abundance organization of these processes in kidney and lung. This should be described as a new systems-level association rather than discovery of a new pathway or protein interaction. Absence from STRING or hu.MAP is not sufficient evidence of novelty, because those resources emphasize curated or experimentally supported interactions and may not capture tissue-level co-abundance. The novelty claim is therefore moderate: the module may represent a previously unreported cross-tissue abundance program, but it requires replication in independent kidney and lung cohorts, control for cell composition, and comparison with published tissue co-expression or proteomic modules before being called a new biological mechanism.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects a shared high-demand cellular state in kidney and lung in which purine and ribonucleotide production supplies nucleic-acid synthesis and energy-related needs, aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases sustain translation, and glycolytic, pentose-phosphate, redox, chaperone, nuclear-transport, and vesicle systems coordinate biosynthetic output and stress adaptation. GART, PAICS, PFAS, GMPS, and MTHFD1 could form the metabolic backbone; AARS1 and the other synthetases could represent translation capacity; PKM, PFKP, G6PD, and TKT could provide carbon flux and reducing power; and HSPA8, HSPH1, RAN, XPO1, HNRNPK, PTBP1, PDCD6IP, COPB1, USO1, and AP1M1 could connect proteostasis, RNA handling, nuclear transport, and vesicle biology. This is a mechanistic hypothesis, not proof of physical assembly or causal regulation. The vesicle and extracellular annotations may reflect release or sampling of intracellular proteins rather than a secretory function of the whole module.

- **Why important**: GART is the highest-positioned network gene in the supplied ranking and connects many module components. It encodes a trifunctional enzyme required for de novo purine biosynthesis and appears across nearly all primary purine, nucleotide, ligase, and binding terms, including (GO:MF ligase activity; q_global = 1.26e-05) and (GO:BP GMP biosynthetic process; q_global = 0.00195). It has strong STRING links to GMPS, MTHFD1, PAICS, and PFAS, and participates in hu.MAP complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_03291.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_00676.1). Its substantial literature and disease-related cancer associations make it a particularly informative metabolic anchor.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports GART as a central purine-biosynthesis component and a major network bridge in this module. It is a strong candidate for representing the module's core metabolic program. It is not established that GART drives the kidney-lung co-abundance pattern or that its protein physically complexes with every connected module member.

Causality, tissue-specific regulation, and direct physical association across the entire module remain hypotheses.

- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports AARS1 as a marker of coordinated translational capacity and aminoacyl-tRNA metabolism. Its network position suggests integration with purine and general biosynthetic activity, but the data do not prove that altered AARS1 activity causes the full module or explains tissue sharing.

Its position is compatible with coordination between biosynthetic capacity and gene-expression control. The extracellular annotation should not be taken as evidence that HNRNPK is secreted, and its role as a driver of the shared tissue module remains unproven.

## Module 5

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (22)**: ADNP, ATRX, BPTF, BRD2, CCDC25, DDX41, GIGYF2, KDM3B, MED1, MTA3, NUP98, PHF3, PIH1D1, PRPF31, PRP4K, MRPS31, SCAF8, SMARCA4, THOC1, THYN1, XRN1, ZMYM3

### Function

The strongest interpretation is a conserved nuclear gene-expression module centered on chromatin regulation, transcription, RNA synthesis, RNA processing, and RNA turnover. This conclusion is supported by the globally corrected globally significant GO/KEGG terms for nucleic-acid binding, RNA metabolic process, nuclear protein-containing complex, nucleic-acid biosynthesis, DNA binding, chromatin binding, nucleic-acid metabolism, RNA biosynthesis, gene expression, and regulation of transcription and RNA metabolism. The convergence of these terms is more informative than any individual annotation: most module genes participate in nuclear information processing rather than in a tissue-specific metabolic or extracellular pathway. The co-abundance network is relatively sparse but has a discernible hub-like organization, with BPTF and NUP98 among the most connected genes and with links bridging chromatin regulators, RNA-processing factors, nuclear transport, and RNA surveillance. The supplementary STRING terms independently support nucleic-acid binding, DNA binding, transcription regulation, nuclear protein complexes, nucleoplasm, chromatin binding, and bromodomain-related biology. These STRING results are supplementary and do not replace the globally corrected globally significant GO/KEGG evidence. The absence of a specific KEGG pathway or a more narrowly defined biological process does not invalidate the module; it suggests a broad regulatory program rather than a single specialized pathway.

**Cell composition.** This is not a plasma-protein or secreted-protein module: the HPA summary reports no secreted genes, and the proteins are predominantly nuclear or nucleoplasmic. The module also does not look like a blood-cell contamination signature. HPA reports low immune-cell specificity for nearly all genes, no enriched blood-cell category, and only isolated cell-type enrichment involving neutrophils or other specialized cell populations. Most genes have low tissue specificity and are detected in both kidney and lung, consistent with broadly expressed housekeeping or core regulatory machinery. A minority show cell-type-enhanced expression, including genes associated with neutrophils, reproductive cell types, esophageal cells, tuft cells, respiratory ciliated cells, or adrenal cortex cells; these isolated annotations do not establish that the shared kidney-lung signal is driven by one of those cell populations. MTA3 is tissue-enhanced in lymphoid tissue, but this is insufficient to redefine the entire module as lymphoid. Overall, the shared module most plausibly reflects conserved cellular gene-regulatory machinery present in kidney and lung, while residual cell-composition effects cannot be excluded.

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides direct or high-confidence support for selected relationships rather than for the whole co-abundance structure. The strongest supplied interaction is PRP4K with PRPF31, consistent with spliceosomal biology. Additional known links connect BPTF with ADNP and ATRX, BPTF with SMARCA4, BRD2 with SMARCA4, and KDM3B with SMARCA4, supporting a chromatin-remodeling and transcription-regulatory subnetwork. STRING functional terms (STRING Function Nucleic acid binding; within-query FDR = 1.98e-05) through (STRING Function Histone binding; within-query FDR = 0.0446) reinforce nuclear, chromatin, transcription, and nucleic-acid functions, but their reported FDRs are within-query supplementary evidence and were not corrected across modules. hu.MAP adds complex-level support for ATRX, ZMYM3, and ADNP in a shared complex context (hu.MAP huMAP3_11539.1), for PRP4K, THOC1, and DDX41 in an RNA-processing-related complex (hu.MAP huMAP3_09943.1), for repeated ZMYM3-ADNP complex overlaps (hu.MAP huMAP3_09582.1) through (hu.MAP huMAP3_10311.1), for BPTF-MTA3 overlaps in NuRD-related complex contexts (hu.MAP huMAP3_09499.1), (hu.MAP huMAP3_10472.1), and (hu.MAP huMAP3_15128.1), and for PRP4K-MRPS31 in (hu.MAP huMAP3_05807.1). These are important because they show that parts of the module correspond to known molecular assemblies. However, most module genes are not linked by a single known complex, and many co-abundance edges lack a supplied STRING or hu.MAP counterpart. Thus the module is best viewed as a coordinated functional neighborhood assembled from several interacting nuclear processes, not as one physical protein complex.

### Novelty

The module-level finding may be novel, but the evidence does not justify claiming that every relationship or every functional association is new. Several edges and subgroups are already supported by STRING or hu.MAP, especially the PRP4K-PRPF31 spliceosomal relationship, the BPTF-ATRX-ADNP-SMARCA4 chromatin-related relationships, the BRD2-SMARCA4 relationship, and the repeated ADNP-ZMYM3 and BPTF-MTA3 complex overlaps. Conversely, the kidney-lung co-abundance organization brings together chromatin remodeling, transcriptional control, nuclear transport, RNA export, splicing, and RNA decay in a pattern not directly represented by the supplied STRING or hu.MAP evidence. Absence from those resources alone does not prove novelty, because databases are incomplete and co-abundance is not equivalent to physical interaction. A careful conclusion is therefore that the biological components are largely established, while the conserved kidney-lung co-abundance module and its particular cross-process organization are a plausible new systems-level observation requiring independent replication and experimental validation.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: this module represents a conserved nuclear gene-expression maintenance program shared by kidney and lung cells. Chromatin regulators and transcriptional cofactors such as BPTF, ATRX, SMARCA4, BRD2, KDM3B, MTA3, ADNP, and MED1 may establish or remodel transcriptionally competent chromatin; THOC1 and NUP98 may couple transcription to nuclear RNA export; PRP4K, PRPF31, DDX41, and related factors may support RNA processing; and XRN1, GIGYF2, and other regulators may control RNA stability or translation. The co-abundance pattern could therefore arise because transcription, chromatin state, RNA processing, export, and RNA turnover are coordinated in cells maintaining epithelial and organ-specific functions. This is a mechanistic model rather than a demonstrated physical pathway. Testing it would require perturbing representative chromatin and RNA-processing genes in kidney- and lung-derived cells and measuring coordinated effects on chromatin accessibility, transcription, splicing, export, and RNA stability.

Its hu.MAP context with PRP4K and MRPS31 (hu.MAP huMAP3_05807.1) further supports association with RNA-processing machinery. It has established disease associations with retinitis pigmentosa and inherited retinal dystrophy and a substantial literature base.
- **Conclusion**: Established: PRPF31 anchors the spliceosomal and RNA-processing interpretation of the module, with concordant functional annotation, network placement, STRING support, and disease biology. Hypothesis: its co-abundance with NUP98 and chromatin regulators may reflect coupling between transcription, nuclear RNA processing, and export.

#### SCAF8

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): CCDC25 (0.83), BPTF (0.82), KDM3B (0.81); STRING partners: none
- **HPA**: Low tissue specificity; Low cell type specificity; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Nucleoplasm; IHC detected in kidney, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: no complex with >= 2 module members
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: SR-related CTD associated factor 8. Enables RNA binding activity and RNA polymerase II C-terminal domain phosphoserine binding activity. Involved in negative regulation of termination of RNA polymerase II transcription, poly(A)-coupled and positive regulation of DNA-templated transcription, elongation. Located in nucleoplasm. [provided by Alliance of Genome Resources, Apr 2022]
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: retinal artery occlusion (0.276); allergic rhinitis (0.269); ankylosing spondylitis (0.255); asthma (0.247); ovarian dysfunction (0.236)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 247
- **Why important**: SCAF8 has a bridging position between CCDC25, BPTF, and KDM3B and appears in nearly all globally significant globally significant GO/KEGG terms. Its established molecular description links RNA binding and RNA polymerase II elongation and termination, making it a particularly useful connector between transcriptional regulation and RNA metabolism. It is detected in both kidney and lung, has low tissue and immune specificity, and has disease associations including asthma and allergic rhinitis, although its literature volume is more limited than that of the major chromatin remodelers.
- **Conclusion**: Established: SCAF8 is a plausible transcription-elongation and RNA-processing connector within a broadly expressed nuclear module. Hypothesis: SCAF8 may help explain why chromatin regulators and RNA-processing factors co-vary across kidney and lung by coupling polymerase elongation to RNA maturation.

## Module 6

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (15)**: ALB, SERPINC1, APOA1, CAT, CLU, C3, COL4A1, COL4A2, COL6A1, COL6A2, FBN1, HBA1, HSPG2, GC, VTN

### Function

The strongest interpretation is a secreted extracellular and basement-membrane module linking extracellular-matrix architecture with circulating lipid, complement, coagulation, and carrier proteins. The primary evidence is the globally corrected enrichment for endoplasmic-reticulum lumen (GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum lumen; q_global = 1.61e-09), blood microparticle (GO:CC blood microparticle; q_global = 2.24e-08), extracellular-matrix structural constituent (GO:MF extracellular matrix structural constituent; q_global = 1.2e-06), extracellular space (GO:CC extracellular space; q_global = 5.86e-06), external encapsulating structure (GO:CC external encapsulating structure; q_global = 7.15e-06), extracellular matrix (GO:CC extracellular matrix; q_global = 7.15e-06), integrin signaling (KEGG Integrin signaling - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 1.05e-05), basement membrane (GO:CC basement membrane; q_global = 2.66e-05), ECM-receptor interaction (KEGG ECM-receptor interaction - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 3.64e-05), complement and coagulation cascades (KEGG Complement and coagulation cascades - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 0.00095), collagen trimers (GO:CC collagen trimer; q_global = 0.00122), focal adhesion (KEGG Focal adhesion - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 0.0139), and related structural and signaling terms. The collagen genes, fibrillin, and perlecan support a structural matrix axis, whereas C3, SERPINC1, VTN, CLU, APOA1, ALB, and GC support complement, coagulation, adhesion, lipid transport, and extracellular carrier functions. Tube development (GO:BP tube development; q_global = 0.0216), receptor-ligand and receptor-regulator activity (GO:MF receptor ligand activity; q_global = 0.0226) (GO:MF signaling receptor activator activity; q_global = 0.0272) (GO:MF signaling receptor regulator activity; q_global = 0.0298), and positive regulation of endocytosis (GO:BP positive regulation of endocytosis; q_global = 0.0415) suggest that the module may also influence vascular or epithelial interfaces. The module therefore appears to represent extracellular matrix and basement-membrane biology integrated with plasma-derived extracellular proteins, rather than a narrowly intracellular pathway. The STRING annotations (15 supplementary STRING terms; within-query FDR 1.51e-13–8.05e-08) independently support secretion, extracellular localization, collagen-containing matrix, and blood microparticle association, but they remain supplementary evidence.

**Cell composition.** The composition strongly cautions against interpreting this as a purely tissue-intrinsic kidney or lung program. Most genes are secreted proteins, with prominent liver and hepatocyte enrichment in HPA, including ALB, APOA1, C3, GC, CLU, SERPINC1, and VTN. Several of these are plasma proteins or blood carriers, and the blood-microparticle signal (GO:CC blood microparticle; q_global = 2.24e-08) is supported by ALB, APOA1, C3, CLU, GC, HBA1, SERPINC1, and VTN. HBA1 is erythrocyte-associated, while several genes show immune-cell enrichment or enhancement in HPA. This pattern is compatible with blood or plasma contribution to kidney and lung samples, including vascular contents, and may also reflect shared vascular or stromal compartments. A genuine extracellular-matrix component is nevertheless present: COL4A1, COL4A2, COL6A1, COL6A2, FBN1, HSPG2, and VTN are associated with matrix, fibroblast, hepatic-stellate, alveolar, or vascular stromal contexts. HPA detects protein in kidney for several genes and in lung for fewer genes, so the shared co-abundance signal should be validated with cell-resolved or tissue-compartment data before assigning it to the same resident cell population in both organs.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 41 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING contains established relationships within two recognizable portions of the module. The plasma-protein portion includes strong links among ALB, APOA1, C3, CLU, GC, SERPINC1, and VTN, consistent with known carrier, complement, coagulation, and extracellular-interaction biology. The matrix portion includes COL4A1-COL4A2, COL6A1-COL6A2, links between type four and type six collagen, and connections involving FBN1, HSPG2, and VTN. These relationships indicate that the network is not composed solely of correlations without prior biological support. hu.MAP provides additional support for a plasma and complement-associated assembly centered on ALB, APOA1, C3, GC, SERPINC1, and HBA1 (hu.MAP huMAP3_15040.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09434.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10678.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07432.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08224.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08395.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09531.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10101.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07265.1), and for collagen-associated assemblies containing COL4A1, COL4A2, and COL6A1 or COL6A2 (hu.MAP huMAP3_06555.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10984.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10699.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_13038.1). However, the hu.MAP matches are partial memberships in broad complexes, not proof that every module protein forms one physical complex. Several matrix genes and HSPG2 lack hu.MAP support in this module, so the network is better viewed as coordinated extracellular biology containing interacting submodules than as a single molecular complex.

### Novelty

The underlying biology is not wholly new: the STRING and hu.MAP evidence recapitulates well-established plasma-protein, complement, coagulation, collagen, basement-membrane, and extracellular-matrix relationships. Absence of some genes from STRING or hu.MAP cannot establish novelty. The potentially new result is the conserved co-abundance organization of these plasma-associated and matrix-associated components across kidney and lung. That conservation could reflect a shared vascular or extracellular compartment, common blood exposure, or coordinated remodeling, but the fact sheet does not establish that this cross-tissue module has not previously been reported. I would therefore describe it as a biologically coherent and potentially useful integrative finding, not as a definitively novel molecular pathway. Independent replication with matched tissue quality, plasma-depletion controls, and cell-type-resolved measurements is needed before claiming discovery of a new tissue mechanism.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module captures a shared vascular-interstitial extracellular environment in kidney and lung. Liver-derived plasma proteins may enter or track with the tissue vasculature, where complement, coagulation regulators, lipoprotein carriers, and adhesion proteins interact with basement-membrane and collagen networks. COL4A1, COL4A2, COL6A1, COL6A2, FBN1, and HSPG2 could provide the structural scaffold, while VTN and related extracellular proteins could connect that scaffold to integrin and focal-adhesion signaling (4 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 1.05e-05–0.0139; see the table). C3, CLU, and SERPINC1 could modulate inflammatory and protease-related activity at the same interface. The co-abundance signal may therefore arise from shared vascular exposure plus matrix remodeling, rather than from a single cell type producing the entire module. This is a hypothesis; the fact sheet does not demonstrate directionality, physical colocalization, or causal regulation.

It is a plausible mechanistic driver, but the co-abundance data alone do not show that COL4A1 initiates remodeling or causes the module.

Its localization in both organs makes it more compatible with a resident vascular or basement-membrane contribution than the liver-enriched plasma proteins. Whether it mediates the cross-talk between matrix remodeling and complement or lipoprotein biology remains a hypothesis.

## Module 7

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (5)**: AQR, TEX10, GTF3C2, GTF3C4, TFIP11

### Function

The strongest interpretation is a nuclear RNA-processing and transcription module. The primary enrichment evidence is highly coherent: GTF3C2 and GTF3C4 occur together in the TFIIIC complex (GO:CC transcription factor TFIIIC complex; q_global = 0.0228), have RNA polymerase three general transcription-initiation activity (GO:MF RNA polymerase III general transcription initiation factor activity; q_global = 0.0228), support five-S ribosomal-RNA transcription (GO:BP 5S class rRNA transcription by RNA polymerase III; q_global = 0.0228), and occur in an RNA polymerase three transcription-regulator complex (GO:CC RNA polymerase III transcription regulator complex; q_global = 0.0228). This is supported by the STRING functional annotations (STRING Component Transcription factor TFIIIC complex; within-query FDR = 0.0335) and (STRING Function RNA polymerase III general transcription initiation factor activity; within-query FDR = 0.0467). A second component is spliceosomal RNA processing, represented by AQR and TFIP11 and supported by their repeated co-membership in hu.MAP spliceosome-related complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_06726.1) through (hu.MAP huMAP3_09945.1). The module therefore most plausibly reflects coordinated nuclear control of small-RNA transcription, ribosome-related RNA production, and pre-mRNA splicing. The GO evidence is concentrated in GTF3C2 and GTF3C4 rather than distributed across all genes, so the broader RNA-processing interpretation should be regarded as integrative rather than as a single globally enriched pathway.

**Cell composition.** The HPA profile argues against the module being driven primarily by plasma proteins, secreted proteins, or blood-cell contamination. No module gene is secreted, all have low tissue specificity, and the blood-cell evidence is low immune-cell specificity or absence from immune cells. Three genes show enhanced association with particular cell types, including cardiomyocytes, epicardial cells, early spermatids, or late primary spermatocytes, but there is no recurring kidney- or lung-specific cell type in the supplied HPA summary. Protein detection is directly reported for only GTF3C2 in kidney and lung, while the other genes lack protein data in those tissues. Thus, the shared kidney–lung signal may reflect broadly distributed nuclear RNA biology, with possible contribution from shared cellular states, rather than a secreted or blood-derived signal.

| GO:MF RNA polymerase III general transcription initiation factor activity | 0.0228 | GTF3C2, GTF3C4 |
| GO:BP 5S class rRNA transcription by RNA polymerase III | 0.0228 | GTF3C2, GTF3C4 |
| GO:CC RNA polymerase III transcription regulator complex | 0.0228 | GTF3C2, GTF3C4 |

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides one high-confidence physical or functional relationship among the module genes: GTF3C2–GTF3C4. This directly reinforces the TFIIIC interpretation and shows that the central transcription pair is already known to be closely connected. STRING also independently assigns the pair to the TFIIIC complex and RNA polymerase three initiation activity (STRING Component Transcription factor TFIIIC complex; within-query FDR = 0.0335) and (STRING Function RNA polymerase III general transcription initiation factor activity; within-query FDR = 0.0467). hu.MAP adds one complex containing GTF3C2 and GTF3C4 (hu.MAP huMAP3_10875.1), and four spliceosome-related complexes containing AQR and TFIP11 (hu.MAP huMAP3_06726.1) through (hu.MAP huMAP3_09945.1). These results support two established molecular groupings inside the module. The cross-group links involving GTF3C4 with AQR and TEX10 are present in the co-abundance network but are not corroborated by the supplied STRING or hu.MAP evidence, so they should be interpreted as co-abundance associations rather than established physical interactions.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 1 of 10 gene pairs: GTF3C2–GTF3C4 (0.993)
**STRING functional enrichment (supplementary, within-query FDR)**: Component Transcription factor TFIIIC complex (FDR = 0.0335, 2 genes); Function RNA polymerase III general transcription initiation factor activity (FDR = 0.0467, 2 genes)
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: huMAP3_10875.1 (GTF3C2, GTF3C4; 2/14); huMAP3_06726.1 (AQR, TFIP11; 2/37); huMAP3_08501.1 (AQR, TFIP11; 2/47); huMAP3_09504.1 (AQR, TFIP11; 2/51); huMAP3_09945.1 (AQR, TFIP11; 2/61)

### Novelty

This module is not a wholly new molecular concept: the TFIIIC association of GTF3C2 with GTF3C4 and the spliceosomal association of AQR with TFIP11 are already represented in STRING and hu.MAP. The potentially novel aspect is the conserved kidney–lung co-abundance arrangement that places these established transcription and splicing components in one small network, including the uncorroborated co-abundance links to TEX10 and between the two functional groupings. However, the supplied evidence does not establish that this tissue-pair module has never been reported. Absence of STRING support for some edges, or absence of a gene pair from hu.MAP, is not sufficient to claim novelty. The defensible conclusion is that the biological components are established, whereas the specific cross-tissue co-abundance organization is a candidate observation requiring independent replication and comparison with published tissue co-expression studies.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the conserved kidney–lung module reflects a coordinated nuclear RNA-production state in which TFIIIC activity, small-RNA and ribosome-related transcription, spliceosome function, and RNA maturation are co-regulated. GTF3C2 and GTF3C4 may define the transcriptional arm, while AQR and TFIP11 define a late spliceosomal arm; TEX10 may connect the network to ribosome-related RNA maturation or broader nuclear RNA homeostasis. This is a mechanistic hypothesis from the co-abundance pattern, not a demonstrated causal pathway. Testing it would require perturbing the transcriptional pair or the spliceosomal pair and measuring small-RNA transcription, pre-mRNA splicing, and cellular effects in relevant kidney and lung cell models.

The literature volume is substantial, and disease-association records include neurodegenerative disease and coronary artery disorder, although these associations do not demonstrate a role in this module.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence identifies GTF3C4 as a TFIIIC and RNA polymerase three transcription component and as a strongly connected member of this network. It is a leading candidate for an organizing node, but the claim that it regulates the kidney–lung module specifically remains a hypothesis.

Its G-patch domain and reported recruitment of a DEAH-box helicase support a role in late-stage spliceosomal disassembly and lariat-intron release. It also has disease-association records involving neurodegenerative disease and skin cancer and a meaningful literature base. It is also a component of the MLL1 complex and shows enhanced HPA association with cardiomyocytes and epicardial cells. Its literature and disease-association records are more limited than those of AQR, but its RNA-maturation annotation provides a biologically plausible link between the TFIIIC-centered transcriptional signal and ribosome-related RNA handling.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports TEX10 in ribosomal-RNA processing and complex biology, but not a direct relationship with GTF3C4 or with the spliceosomal genes in the supplied databases. Its proposed bridging role in this module is therefore the most tentative of the selected interpretations.

## Module 8

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (25)**: ARID2, BOD1L1, BRD8, DDX55, DHX37, DHX8, INTS14, INTS7, ISY1, NF1, NSMCE3, NUP88, PAXBP1, PPHLN1, RBM33, POLR2A, POLR3A, SDAD1, SENP3, SRCAP, SUGP1, SUGP2, TASOR, TRIM24, ZNF326

### Function

The module is best interpreted as a conserved nuclear gene-expression and RNA-processing module. The primary evidence is the globally corrected enrichment for nucleoplasm, nuclear lumen, nucleus, nucleic-acid metabolism, gene expression, nuclear protein-containing complexes, RNA metabolism, RNA biosynthesis, macromolecule biosynthesis, and related nucleobase-containing compound metabolism (14 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 5.09e-05–0.0419; see the table). The genes span transcription by RNA polymerases, chromatin remodeling, spliceosome function, RNA helicase activity, RNA export, ribosome biogenesis, nucleocytoplasmic transport, and genome-maintenance processes. The co-abundance structure therefore appears to reflect coordinated abundance of a broad nuclear information-processing program rather than one narrowly defined biochemical pathway. The STRING enrichment is concordant supplementary evidence (8 supplementary STRING terms; within-query FDR 4.11e-05–0.0312), but the globally corrected GO evidence is the basis for this conclusion.

**Cell composition.** This is not a plasma-protein or secreted-protein module: HPA reports no secreted genes, and the proteins are predominantly intracellular, especially nuclear or nucleolar. The module is also not strongly blood-cell enriched. Most genes have low tissue specificity and low immune-cell specificity, while a smaller subset shows cell-type-enhanced patterns involving diverse specialized cell types. Protein detection is reported for most module genes in both kidney and lung, supporting a shared cellular program, although the absence of detection for some genes and the heterogeneous single-cell patterns mean that cell composition and technical effects cannot be excluded. The shared module is therefore more consistent with broadly deployed nuclear housekeeping or gene-regulatory activity than with a blood-derived signal.
