### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING independently supports a nuclear interpretation through nucleoplasm, nuclear lumen, nucleus, nuclear protein-complex, transcription, and chromosome annotations (8 supplementary STRING terms; within-query FDR 4.11e-05–0.0312). Several high-confidence pairwise links are already known, including BRD8–SRCAP, DDX55–SDAD1, DHX8–ISY1, INTS14–INTS7, INTS7–POLR2A, and PPHLN1–TASOR. hu.MAP provides additional evidence that subsets of the module coincide with established spliceosome-associated complexes containing DHX8, ISY1, and PAXBP1; chromatin-remodeling complexes containing SRCAP and BRD8; RNA-binding complexes containing ZNF326, SUGP2, and PPHLN1; and RNA-polymerase-associated complexes containing POLR2A and POLR3A (hu.MAP huMAP3_07581.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06726.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08501.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09504.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09945.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07400.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_03019.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09291.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08487.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06426.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10481.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_14306.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10625.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_14318.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_11092.1). Thus, the module is not a collection of unrelated proteins: it contains recognizable submodules for transcription, chromatin regulation, RNA splicing, and RNA polymerase function. At the same time, only a minority of possible within-module pairs have high-confidence STRING support, and most module genes lack a listed direct STRING partner. Co-abundance therefore extends beyond currently catalogued physical interactions and should not be interpreted as a single experimentally established complex.

### Novelty

The module should not be called wholly new. Its principal biological themes and several of its relationships are already well established in STRING and hu.MAP, especially the spliceosome-associated, chromatin-remodeling, integrator-related, RNA-polymerase, and TASOR–PPHLN1 relationships. The potentially novel aspect is the coordinated co-abundance of these partially connected nuclear subsystems as a conserved kidney–lung module. That observation may represent a tissue-shared regulatory state or coordinated cellular program, but novelty cannot be established from absence of a STRING or hu.MAP edge alone. Demonstrating novelty would require comparison with prior tissue co-abundance modules and independent replication in additional cohorts, followed by experimental testing of whether the abundance relationships change together under defined perturbations.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module represents a coordinated nuclear surveillance and gene-expression program in kidney and lung cells. A plausible mechanism is that chromatin regulators and remodelers, including ARID2, SRCAP, BRD8, TRIM24, and TASOR, establish or maintain transcriptional states; POLR2A and POLR3A execute messenger-RNA and small-RNA transcription; INTS7 and INTS14 support RNA-polymerase-associated processing; and DHX8, ISY1, SUGP1, SUGP2, and PAXBP1 coordinate splicing and RNA maturation. DDX55, DHX37, SDAD1, and SENP3 could connect RNA and ribosome biogenesis with nuclear quality control, while NUP88 could support nucleocytoplasmic trafficking. The shared abundance pattern may therefore arise when kidney and lung cells require synchronized control of transcription, RNA processing, chromatin state, and genome or RNA surveillance. This is a mechanistic model, not proof of direct physical cooperation among all module members.

- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports SUGP1 as an RNA-processing component that helps explain the module's breadth across transcription and RNA metabolism. Its position suggests that it may couple splicing-related abundance to chromatin and transcriptional regulation, but a direct bridging mechanism in kidney or lung has not been demonstrated by this fact sheet.

## Module 9

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (18)**: ASH2L, ATXN2L, BAZ2A, CHAMP1, EHMT1, MCM3AP, KLC1, POGZ, PRRC2A, PRRC2C, POLR3D, PPAN, DNTTIP2, TTC5, TXLNA, UTP14A, ZC3H13, ZGPAT

### Function

The module is best interpreted as a broadly expressed nuclear and cytosolic gene-expression module centered on nucleic-acid handling, RNA processing, ribosome biogenesis, chromatin regulation, and genome maintenance. The strongest primary evidence is (GO:MF nucleic acid binding; q_global = 0.0186), in which most module genes are annotated with nucleic-acid binding. The convergence of (STRING Function Nucleic acid binding; within-query FDR = 0.0191) with nuclear localization (STRING Keyword Nucleus; within-query FDR = 0.0302), ubiquitin-related annotations (STRING Keyword Ubl conjugation; within-query FDR = 0.0302) (STRING Keyword Isopeptide bond; within-query FDR = 0.0302), RNA-processing functions, ribosome-biogenesis functions, chromatin regulation, and DNA-damage responses supports a coordinated gene-expression and RNA-metabolism program rather than a narrowly tissue-specific pathway. The network is not a single dense physical-interaction complex: it contains a highly connected RNA-associated core around PRRC2C, with additional branches involving MCM3AP, ZC3H13, ASH2L, UTP14A, and chromatin or chromosome-regulatory genes. Thus, the module likely represents coordinated abundance of proteins participating in connected aspects of nuclear information processing and cellular maintenance.

**Cell composition.** This is not a plasma-protein, secreted-protein, or blood-cell-enriched module. HPA reports no secreted genes, low immune-cell specificity for nearly all genes, and no enriched blood-cell category. Most genes have low tissue specificity and are detected at the protein level in kidney and lung, which is consistent with a broadly expressed housekeeping-like program. The presence of cell-type-enhanced annotations in diverse cell types, including podocytes, respiratory ciliated cells, hepatocytes, plasma cells, germ-cell populations, and glial or neuronal populations, does not define a coherent kidney- or lung-specific cell type. The shared signal may therefore reflect conserved intracellular functions present across multiple resident cell types, although sample cellular composition and common cell states cannot be excluded.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 1 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

| Term | q_global | genes |
|---|---|---|
| GO:MF nucleic acid binding | 0.0186 | ASH2L, ATXN2L, BAZ2A, DNTTIP2, MCM3AP, POGZ, POLR3D, PPAN, PRRC2A, PRRC2C, TTC5, UTP14A, ZC3H13, ZGPAT |

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides direct high-confidence support for only a small set of pairwise relationships within the module: CHAMP1 with POGZ, DNTTIP2 with UTP14A, and PPAN with UTP14A. These links support chromosome regulation and nucleolar or ribosome-biogenesis substructures, but they do not establish that the entire module is one physical complex. STRING functional enrichment independently agrees with nucleic-acid binding and nuclear localization (STRING Function Nucleic acid binding; within-query FDR = 0.0191) (STRING Keyword Nucleus; within-query FDR = 0.0302), while the ubiquitin-related terms (STRING Keyword Ubl conjugation; within-query FDR = 0.0302) (STRING Keyword Isopeptide bond; within-query FDR = 0.0302) are supplementary and not globally corrected primary evidence. hu.MAP supports several recurring relationships involving POGZ and CHAMP1, including a complex containing EHMT1 (hu.MAP huMAP3_11539.1) and additional complexes containing POGZ and CHAMP1 (hu.MAP huMAP3_07496.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_03699.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09963.1). hu.MAP also supports the PRRC2A–PRRC2C pair (hu.MAP huMAP3_08887.1). The repeated POGZ–CHAMP1 and PRRC2A–PRRC2C memberships suggest real molecular subassemblies embedded in the co-abundance module, whereas the absence of curated STRING or hu.MAP links for many other edges indicates that co-abundance extends beyond currently catalogued physical interactions.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 3 of 153 gene pairs: CHAMP1–POGZ (0.907); DNTTIP2–UTP14A (0.987); PPAN–UTP14A (0.796)
**STRING functional enrichment (supplementary, within-query FDR)**: Function Nucleic acid binding (FDR = 0.0191, 14 genes); Keyword Nucleus (FDR = 0.0302, 15 genes); Keyword Ubl conjugation (FDR = 0.0302, 11 genes); Keyword Isopeptide bond (FDR = 0.0302, 10 genes)
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: huMAP3_11539.1 (POGZ, CHAMP1, EHMT1; 3/32); huMAP3_08887.1 (PRRC2A, PRRC2C; 2/2); huMAP3_07496.1 (POGZ, CHAMP1; 2/5); huMAP3_03699.1 (POGZ, CHAMP1; 2/6); huMAP3_09963.1 (POGZ, CHAMP1; 2/16)

### Novelty

The module should not be called a newly discovered molecular complex. Several of its relationships and subassemblies are already represented in STRING and hu.MAP, and its functional themes are established cellular biology. However, the specific conserved co-abundance pattern shared by kidney and lung may still be a new systems-level observation. The evidence supports novelty at the level of tissue-pair co-abundance organization, not novelty of the individual genes, functions, or all pairwise interactions. Absence of many module relationships from STRING or hu.MAP is compatible with incomplete interaction coverage and does not by itself demonstrate novelty. A stronger novelty claim would require comparison with published kidney–lung co-abundance modules, independent cohorts, and experimental validation.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects a conserved intracellular maintenance program in kidney and lung cells that couples RNA maturation and translation control with nucleolar ribosome production, chromatin-state regulation, DNA-damage handling, and chromosome segregation. In this model, the PRRC2C-centered RNA or stress-granule-associated branch is coordinated with the UTP14A–DNTTIP2–PPAN nucleolar branch and with the POGZ–CHAMP1–EHMT1 chromatin and chromosome-regulatory branch. Shared abundance could arise because cells experiencing similar growth, differentiation, or stress states jointly adjust these processes. This is a mechanistic hypothesis rather than a demonstrated physical pathway; the co-abundance data do not establish directionality, direct binding, or causal regulation.

## Module 10

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (14)**: ATAD3A, BRIX1, CCAR1, HCCS, CPSF3, DDX46, FUS, TMPO, TMPO, NUP155, SRRT, SYMPK, TCERG1, YTHDF2

### Function

This is a conserved nuclear RNA-processing and post-transcriptional gene-regulation module. The strongest primary evidence is (GO:BP RNA processing; q_global = 0.00335) RNA processing and (GO:BP mRNA metabolic process; q_global = 0.0417) mRNA metabolic process, with substantial overlap from module genes. The genes include factors involved in pre-mRNA splicing, transcriptional elongation, RNA export, RNA surveillance or decay, ribosome biogenesis, and messenger-RNA three-prime-end processing. The module therefore most plausibly represents coordinated expression of RNA maturation machinery rather than a tissue-specific metabolic or extracellular pathway. The mitochondrial genes ATAD3A and HCCS indicate a possible connection to cellular growth, mitochondrial function, or respiratory state, but they do not outweigh the coherent RNA-processing signal.

**Cell composition.** The module is not dominated by plasma proteins, secreted proteins, or blood-cell-enriched proteins: the HPA summary reports no secreted genes and low immune-cell specificity throughout. Most genes have low tissue specificity, and protein detection is reported in both kidney and lung for nearly all genes with available data. This supports a broadly expressed intracellular module and makes a blood-contamination explanation less likely. The cell-type-enhanced annotations, especially for germ-cell and reproductive cell types, may reflect shared RNA-processing programs or annotation bias rather than a kidney-lung-specific cell population. NUP155 is tissue-enriched in testis, while TMPO is tissue-enhanced in lymphoid tissue, so a small minority of genes may have more specialized expression. BRIX1 lacks kidney and lung IHC detection in the supplied summary, and NUP155 also lacks detection in those tissues; these observations warrant caution when interpreting the module as a uniform protein complex.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 2 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

| Term | q_global | genes |
|---|---|---|
| GO:BP RNA processing | 0.00335 | BRIX1, CPSF3, DDX46, FUS, NUP155, SRRT, SYMPK, TCERG1, YTHDF2 |
| GO:BP mRNA metabolic process | 0.0417 | CPSF3, DDX46, FUS, SRRT, SYMPK, TCERG1, YTHDF2 |

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides known high-confidence relationships within the RNA-processing portion of the module. CCAR1 connects with SRRT and TCERG1; TCERG1 connects with DDX46 and SRRT; and CPSF3 connects very strongly with SYMPK. The STRING enrichment terms (STRING RCTM Processing of Capped Intron-Containing Pre-mRNA; within-query FDR = 1.04e-05) and (STRING RCTM mRNA Splicing - Major Pathway; within-query FDR = 0.0058) support capped intron-containing pre-mRNA processing and major-pathway mRNA splicing, while (STRING Keyword Isopeptide bond; within-query FDR = 0.0111) and (STRING Keyword Ubl conjugation; within-query FDR = 0.0265) suggest ubiquitin-like or isopeptide-related annotations; the latter are supplementary because their FDR was not corrected across modules. hu.MAP provides partial complex-level support: SYMPK and CPSF3 co-occur in (hu.MAP huMAP3_09096.1) and (hu.MAP huMAP3_11757.1), which contain broader cleavage and polyadenylation machinery, while ATAD3A and YTHDF2 co-occur in (hu.MAP huMAP3_12600.1). Most module members do not have a reported hu.MAP complex or STRING partner in this fact sheet. Thus the network contains a well-supported RNA-processing core, but it is not itself a fully established single protein complex.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 5 of 91 gene pairs: CCAR1–SRRT (0.857); CCAR1–TCERG1 (0.92); CPSF3–SYMPK (0.999); DDX46–TCERG1 (0.927); SRRT–TCERG1 (0.839)
**STRING functional enrichment (supplementary, within-query FDR)**: RCTM Processing of Capped Intron-Containing Pre-mRNA (FDR = 1.04e-05, 8 genes); RCTM mRNA Splicing - Major Pathway (FDR = 0.0058, 5 genes); Keyword Isopeptide bond (FDR = 0.0111, 9 genes); Keyword Ubl conjugation (FDR = 0.0265, 9 genes)
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: huMAP3_12600.1 (ATAD3A, YTHDF2; 2/10); huMAP3_09096.1 (SYMPK, CPSF3; 2/15); huMAP3_11757.1 (SYMPK, CPSF3; 2/16)

### Novelty

The broad biological theme is not a new discovery because RNA processing, splicing, and messenger-RNA three-prime-end maturation are established cellular functions, and several within-module relationships are already represented in STRING and hu.MAP. The potentially novel aspect is the conserved kidney-lung co-abundance configuration: the particular combination of RNA-processing, nuclear-envelope, mitochondrial, and RNA-surveillance genes may represent a tissue-pair-specific abundance program. That claim remains provisional. Missing STRING or hu.MAP relationships cannot establish novelty, and co-abundance does not by itself demonstrate direct physical interaction or a new molecular complex. A stronger novelty claim would require comparison with published kidney-lung co-abundance modules, independent cohorts, and controls for shared cell composition and generalized housekeeping expression.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects a shared intracellular RNA-homeostasis program in kidney and lung cells, in which transcriptional elongation and spliceosome-associated factors coordinate with nuclear export, three-prime-end processing, ribosome biogenesis, and messenger-RNA turnover. TCERG1, DDX46, SRRT, CCAR1, CPSF3, and SYMPK may form the principal functional axis, while NUP155 and TMPO could couple RNA transport and nuclear-envelope organization to this program. ATAD3A and HCCS may mark a linked mitochondrial or energetic state, and YTHDF2 may connect transcript modification or decay to the processing network. This is a mechanistic hypothesis from co-abundance and annotations, not proof that all members physically interact or that the module is driven by a single pathway.

#### TCERG1

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): DDX46 (0.85), SRRT (0.83), CCAR1 (0.82), FUS (0.81); STRING partners: CCAR1 (0.92), DDX46 (0.927), SRRT (0.839)
- **HPA**: Low tissue specificity; Low cell type specificity; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Nucleoplasm; IHC detected in kidney, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: no complex with >= 2 module members
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: transcription elongation regulator 1. This gene encodes a nuclear protein that regulates transcriptional elongation and pre-mRNA splicing. The encoded protein interacts with the hyperphosphorylated C-terminal domain of RNA polymerase II via multiple FF domains, and with the pre-mRNA splicing factor SF1 via a WW domain. Alternative splicing results in multiple transcripts variants encoding different isoforms. [provided by RefSeq, Jul 2…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: neurodegenerative disease (0.527); colorectal cancer (0.336); smoking initiation (0.302); sleep apnea syndrome (0.293); chronic venous hypertension (0.257)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 505
- **Why important**: TCERG1 is among the most connected and strongest nodes in the module. It is present in both primary terms (GO:BP RNA processing; q_global = 0.00335) and (GO:BP mRNA metabolic process; q_global = 0.0417) and all the supplementary STRING terms (STRING RCTM Processing of Capped Intron-Containing Pre-mRNA; within-query FDR = 1.04e-05) through (STRING Keyword Ubl conjugation; within-query FDR = 0.0265). Its established functions link transcriptional elongation with pre-mRNA splicing, and its high-confidence STRING relationships with CCAR1, DDX46, and SRRT place it at the center of the RNA-processing subnetwork. Its disease associations include neurodegenerative disease and colorectal cancer, and its literature support is substantial.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports TCERG1 as a central coordinator or marker of the module's transcription and splicing program. It is a strong mechanistic candidate, but the co-abundance data do not prove that TCERG1 regulates the other module genes in kidney or lung.

#### DDX46

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): TCERG1 (0.85), NUP155 (0.81), SRRT (0.81), CCAR1 (0.80); STRING partners: TCERG1 (0.927)
- **HPA**: Low tissue specificity; Low cell type specificity; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Nuclear speckles; IHC detected in kidney, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: no complex with >= 2 module members
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: DEAD-box helicase 46. This gene encodes a member of the DEAD box protein family. DEAD box proteins, characterized by the conserved motif Asp-Glu-Ala-Asp (DEAD), are putative RNA helicases. They are implicated in a number of cellular processes involving alteration of RNA secondary structure, such as translation initiation, nuclear and mitochondrial splicing, and ribosome and spliceosome assembly. Based on their distribu…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: neurodegenerative disease (0.526); dengue disease (0.370); Hypercholesterolemia (0.203); lung adenocarcinoma (0.074); osteosarcoma (0.053)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 592
- **Why important**: DDX46 is one of the most connected and strongest nodes. It appears in (GO:BP RNA processing; q_global = 0.00335) and (GO:BP mRNA metabolic process; q_global = 0.0417), in the splicing-related STRING terms (STRING RCTM Processing of Capped Intron-Containing Pre-mRNA; within-query FDR = 1.04e-05) and (STRING RCTM mRNA Splicing - Major Pathway; within-query FDR = 0.0058), and has a high-confidence STRING relationship with TCERG1. Its established DEAD-box helicase activity and annotations in mRNA splicing and U2-type prespliceosome assembly provide a direct molecular explanation for the module's primary enrichment. It has substantial literature support and disease associations including neurodegenerative disease.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence identifies DDX46 as a plausible core spliceosome-associated component of the module. Its central position supports an important role in the inferred RNA-processing program, while any kidney-lung-specific function remains a hypothesis.

#### SRRT

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): TCERG1 (0.83), CCAR1 (0.83), DDX46 (0.81); STRING partners: CCAR1 (0.857), TCERG1 (0.839)
- **HPA**: Low tissue specificity; Cell type enhanced; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Nucleoplasm; IHC detected in kidney, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: no complex with >= 2 module members
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: serrate, RNA effector molecule. Enables mRNA cap binding complex binding activity and protein-macromolecule adaptor activity. Involved in primary miRNA processing. Located in nucleoplasm. Part of ribonucleoprotein complex. [provided by Alliance of Genome Resources, Apr 2022]
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: dengue disease (0.370); myxedema (0.228); hypothyroidism (0.228); neurodegenerative disease (0.227); hair color (0.175)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 413
- **Why important**: SRRT is connected to TCERG1, CCAR1, and DDX46 and appears in both primary enrichment terms (GO:BP RNA processing; q_global = 0.00335) and (GO:BP mRNA metabolic process; q_global = 0.0417) as well as all supplementary STRING terms. It is an established nuclear adaptor involved in primary microRNA processing and mRNA cap-binding-complex interactions, providing a bridge between pre-mRNA handling and broader RNA regulation. HPA places it in the nucleoplasm and reports cell-type enhancement in spermatogenic populations. Its disease and literature evidence is meaningful, though less extensive than that for FUS.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports SRRT as a multifunctional RNA-processing adaptor within the module. Its role may be to connect splicing-related interactions with RNA surveillance or regulatory processing, but that integrative role is inferential here.

#### CPSF3

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): BRIX1 (0.81); STRING partners: SYMPK (0.999)
- **HPA**: Low tissue specificity; Cell type enhanced; blood Low immune cell specificity; location None; IHC detected in kidney, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: huMAP3_09096.1, huMAP3_11757.1
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: cleavage and polyadenylation specific factor 3. This gene encodes a member of the metallo-beta-lactamase family. The encoded protein is a 73kDa subunit of the cleavage and polyadenylation specificity factor and functions as an endonuclease that recognizes the pre-mRNA 3'-cleavage site AAUAAA prior to polyadenylation. It also cleaves after the pre-mRNA sequence ACCCA during histone 3'-end pre-mRNA processing. [provided by RefSeq, Oct 2012].
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: neurodevelopmental disorder with microcephaly, hypotonia, nystagmus, and seizures (0.601); neurodegenerative disease (0.527); neurodevelopmental disorder with hypotonia, microcephaly, and seizures (0.416); complex neurodevelopmental disorder (0.370); dengue disease (0.370)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 548
- **Why important**: CPSF3 is the catalytic endonuclease of the cleavage and polyadenylation specificity factor and is annotated for mRNA three-prime-end processing and histone mRNA processing. It occurs in (GO:BP RNA processing; q_global = 0.00335) and (GO:BP mRNA metabolic process; q_global = 0.0417), participates in (STRING RCTM Processing of Capped Intron-Containing Pre-mRNA; within-query FDR = 1.04e-05), (STRING Keyword Isopeptide bond; within-query FDR = 0.0111), and (STRING Keyword Ubl conjugation; within-query FDR = 0.0265), and has an exceptionally strong STRING relationship with SYMPK. It also appears with SYMPK in hu.MAP complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_09096.1) and (hu.MAP huMAP3_11757.1), which contain additional polyadenylation machinery. Its disease associations include neurodevelopmental disorders and neurodegenerative disease.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports CPSF3 as a functional anchor for the module's mRNA maturation component. The network and hu.MAP evidence indicate a genuine cleavage and polyadenylation submodule, although the entire co-abundance module extends beyond that machinery.

#### FUS

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): TCERG1 (0.81), TMPO (0.80); STRING partners: none
- **HPA**: Low tissue specificity; Low cell type specificity; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Nucleoplasm; IHC detected in kidney, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: no complex with >= 2 module members
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: FUS RNA binding protein. This gene encodes a multifunctional protein component of the heterogeneous nuclear ribonucleoprotein (hnRNP) complex. The hnRNP complex is involved in pre-mRNA splicing and the export of fully processed mRNA to the cytoplasm. This protein belongs to the FET family of RNA-binding proteins which have been implicated in cellular processes that include regulation of gene expression, maintenance of gen…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (0.710); sporadic amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (0.683); frontotemporal dementia with motor neuron disease (0.667); essential tremor (0.557); juvenile amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (0.527)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 19869
- **Why important**: FUS is present in both primary terms (GO:BP RNA processing; q_global = 0.00335) and (GO:BP mRNA metabolic process; q_global = 0.0417) and all supplementary STRING terms. It is a multifunctional RNA-binding protein involved in heterogeneous nuclear ribonucleoprotein complexes, pre-mRNA splicing, RNA export, RNA stabilization, and gene regulation. Although its graph connectivity is more limited than that of TCERG1 or DDX46, it is biologically influential and has very extensive literature support, with strong disease associations for amyotrophic lateral sclerosis and frontotemporal dementia with motor neuron disease.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports FUS as an important RNA-processing and RNA-stability component of the module. Its disease relevance makes it biologically important, but the module does not establish that the kidney-lung co-abundance pattern is related to FUS-associated neurodegeneration.

## Module 11

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (48)**: ATXN10, CBS, CIAO1, DAZAP1, DYNC1I2, DDX5, DDX39B, EIF3C, EIF3F, GEMIN4, HDAC1, HNRNPH1, HNRNPF, EIF4G1, EIF6, IMPDH2, MED31, NAA10, NOL10, OLA1, PSMC4, PSMC2, ATIC, RBBP4, RBBP7, RBM39, RPL3, RPL6, RPL9, RUVBL2, SF3B3, SRP68, SRP72, DARS1, EPRS1, IARS1, KARS1, LARS1, QARS1, RARS1, YARS1, CCT5, CCT3, CCT7, CCT8, SNRNP200, EFTUD2, UBE2N

### Function

The module is best interpreted as a conserved intracellular gene-expression and protein-biosynthesis program. Its strongest primary evidence is coordinated aminoacyl-tRNA charging through the aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase multienzyme complex and aminoacyl-tRNA biosynthesis (GO:CC aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase multienzyme complex; q_global = 9.16e-07) (GO:MF aminoacyl-tRNA ligase activity; q_global = 8.91e-05) (GO:BP tRNA aminoacylation for protein translation; q_global = 8.91e-05) (KEGG Aminoacyl-tRNA biosynthesis - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 0.000164). A second major component is RNA handling: ribonucleoprotein complexes, RNA binding, RNA localization, RNA metabolism, spliceosomal catalysis, and protein-RNA complex assembly are globally supported (GO:CC ribonucleoprotein complex; q_global = 1.26e-06) (GO:MF RNA binding; q_global = 3.1e-05) (GO:BP RNA localization to Cajal body; q_global = 3.8e-05) (GO:BP RNA localization to nucleus; q_global = 3.8e-05) (GO:MF nucleic acid binding; q_global = 0.000242) (GO:BP RNA splicing, via transesterification reactions with bulged adenosine as nucleophile; q_global = 0.00564) (GO:BP mRNA splicing, via spliceosome; q_global = 0.00564) (GO:BP protein-RNA complex assembly; q_global = 0.00656) (GO:BP RNA metabolic process; q_global = 0.0415). Translation, protein biosynthesis, cytosolic ribosomal components, chaperonin-mediated folding, proteasomal activity, and nucleotide-dependent processes are also represented (GO:CC chaperonin-containing T-complex; q_global = 0.000272) (GO:BP translation; q_global = 0.000781) (GO:BP protein biosynthetic process; q_global = 0.000781) (GO:CC protein folding chaperone complex; q_global = 0.00822) (GO:MF ATP hydrolysis activity; q_global = 0.0191) (GO:MF ATP-dependent protein folding chaperone; q_global = 0.0452) (GO:CC cytosolic large ribosomal subunit; q_global = 0.0482). Thus, the module is not a tissue-specific signaling module; it is a coordinated housekeeping-like program linking tRNA charging, translation, RNA processing, folding, and protein quality control. The Cajal-body and telomerase-related terms (GO:BP RNA localization to Cajal body; q_global = 3.8e-05) (GO:BP telomerase RNA localization to Cajal body; q_global = 3.8e-05) (GO:BP telomerase RNA localization; q_global = 3.8e-05) (GO:BP regulation of telomerase RNA localization to Cajal body; q_global = 0.000272) (GO:BP positive regulation of telomerase RNA localization to Cajal body; q_global = 0.000272) (GO:BP positive regulation of telomere maintenance via telomerase; q_global = 0.00761) (GO:BP positive regulation of telomere maintenance; q_global = 0.0246) (GO:BP regulation of telomere maintenance via telomerase; q_global = 0.0343) are driven largely by the CCT subunits and should be interpreted as a more focused subprogram within this broader proteostasis and RNA-processing context.

**Cell composition.** The HPA evidence argues against a plasma-protein, secreted-protein, or blood-cell explanation. Only one module gene is annotated as secreted, and almost all genes have low immune-cell specificity; the module therefore does not resemble a blood-cell-enriched module. Most genes have low tissue specificity and are detected at the protein level in both kidney and lung, supporting a broadly expressed intracellular program shared by these tissues. There are cell-type-enhanced signals involving reproductive, epithelial, ciliated, trophoblast, neural, and other cell types, but they are heterogeneous rather than a coherent kidney or lung cell-type signature. The HPA pattern is therefore more consistent with shared cellular machinery, possibly influenced by common epithelial or ciliated-cell content, than with plasma contamination or a single dominant blood population. The lack of kidney or lung protein detection for selected genes, including RARS1 and CBS, is a caveat against assuming uniform activity of every module member in every sample.

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides strong prior support for several recognizable substructures. The aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase group forms a highly connected cluster containing DARS1, EPRS1, IARS1, KARS1, LARS1, QARS1, RARS1, and YARS1, consistent with the aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase multienzyme complex (STRING Component aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase multienzyme complex; within-query FDR = 1.05e-06). CCT3, CCT5, CCT7, and CCT8 form the CCT chaperonin cluster, with additional links to RUVBL2 and translation-related proteins. DDX5, DDX39B, EFTUD2, HNRNPF, HNRNPH1, RBM39, SF3B3, and SNRNP200 support known RNA-processing and spliceosomal relationships (STRING Component Ribonucleoprotein complex; within-query FDR = 4.34e-09) (STRING COMPARTMENTS Ribonucleoprotein complex; within-query FDR = 2.42e-07). EIF3C, EIF3F, EIF4G1, EIF6, ribosomal proteins, and SRP68/SRP72 support translation, ribonucleoprotein, and cotranslational targeting relationships (STRING RCTM Translation; within-query FDR = 1.29e-09) (STRING Component Ribonucleoprotein complex; within-query FDR = 4.34e-09) (STRING Process Translation; within-query FDR = 1.74e-06). The hu.MAP results independently recover the aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase complex (hu.MAP huMAP3_02637.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_13688.1), the CCT complex (hu.MAP huMAP3_07938.1) through (hu.MAP huMAP3_14584.1), spliceosomal assemblies (hu.MAP huMAP3_08980.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_00960.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08501.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09504.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09945.1), translation-initiation assemblies (hu.MAP huMAP3_13175.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_11246.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_01276.1), chromatin and deacetylase assemblies (hu.MAP huMAP3_15128.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08800.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09031.1), and proteasome assemblies (hu.MAP huMAP3_05064.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09656.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_13725.1). These known relationships mean that much of the module reflects genuine molecular complexes rather than arbitrary co-abundance. At the same time, STRING and hu.MAP do not establish that all module edges are direct physical interactions or that the complete module is one biochemical complex.

### Novelty

The module should not be claimed as a wholly new molecular complex. Its central components are already well established in STRING and hu.MAP, including aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase, CCT, spliceosomal, translation-initiation, chromatin-regulatory, and proteasome assemblies. The potentially novel observation is the conserved kidney-lung co-abundance organization that brings these established intracellular subsystems together in one tissue-pair module. That organization may represent a new systems-level signature, but the fact sheet does not provide cross-study replication, perturbation evidence, or comparison with published kidney-lung modules sufficient to establish discovery-level novelty. Absence of hu.MAP or STRING support for individual genes such as DDX5, RUVBL2, or IMPDH2 does not by itself demonstrate novelty, because database coverage and tissue co-abundance are different types of evidence.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: in shared kidney and lung cell populations, cellular demand for protein production and proteome maintenance coordinately raises aminoacyl-tRNA charging, translation initiation, ribosome-associated functions, RNA splicing and export, chaperonin folding, and proteasome-dependent quality control. The aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase cluster may supply charged tRNAs for translation, while EIF3 and ribosomal components support initiation and elongation; spliceosomal factors may regulate the transcript supply, CCT proteins may fold newly synthesized proteins, and proteasome components may remove damaged or misassembled proteins. RUVBL2, DDX5, and the CCT subunits could provide bridges between RNA processing, chaperoning, and nuclear or cytosolic protein maintenance. This is a mechanistic model rather than a demonstrated causal pathway; testing would require cell-type-resolved data, perturbation experiments, and independent replication across kidney and lung cohorts.

## Module 12

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (13)**: HSPA5, MTHFD1L, DNAJC10, FLNB, GANAB, HYOU1, MOGS, PDIA4, PRKDC, QSOX2, SCFD1, SEPTIN9, UGGT1

### Function

The module is strongly associated with endoplasmic-reticulum protein quality control and maturation. The primary evidence is the globally corrected enrichment for protein processing in the endoplasmic reticulum (KEGG Protein processing in endoplasmic reticulum - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 5.29e-06), protein folding (GO:BP protein folding; q_global = 3.22e-05), the endoplasmic-reticulum chaperone complex (GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum chaperone complex; q_global = 0.000927), protein maturation (GO:BP protein maturation; q_global = 0.0018), the endoplasmic-reticulum lumen (GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum lumen; q_global = 0.00301), and endoplasmic-reticulum protein-containing complexes (GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum protein-containing complex; q_global = 0.0137). The overlapping genes indicate coordinated functions in chaperoning, disulfide-bond processing, N-linked glycan processing, recognition of incompletely folded glycoproteins, and handling of misfolded proteins. The network is not merely a collection of loosely related ER genes: its central region links HSPA5, HYOU1, PDIA4, GANAB, DNAJC10, and UGGT1, while MOGS and QSOX2 provide additional glycan-processing and oxidative-folding connections. PRKDC, MTHFD1L, SEPTIN9, FLNB, and SCFD1 are less directly supported by the enrichment, suggesting that the module may contain peripheral or tissue-contextual relationships in addition to its ER-proteostasis core.

**Cell composition.** The module does not appear to be primarily a plasma-protein or blood-cell module. HPA reports low immune-cell specificity for nearly all genes, no repeatedly enriched blood-cell category, and one gene not detected in immune cells. Several genes are annotated as intracellular or membrane-associated rather than secreted, although the aggregate HPA summary identifies a substantial secreted-gene component. Most genes have low tissue specificity but enhanced specificity in particular cell types, including plasma cells, trophoblasts, reproductive cells, urothelial cells, and erythrocyte progenitors. This pattern is compatible with broadly distributed ER and secretory-pathway biology, with possible contributions from shared secretory or specialized epithelial cell states, rather than a simple blood-contamination explanation. Protein detection is observed in kidney and lung for nearly all genes, but HYOU1 is not detected by the cited HPA protein assay in either tissue; this cautions against interpreting the module as uniformly abundant protein in both organs.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 6 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

|---|---|---|

| GO:BP protein folding | 3.22e-05 | DNAJC10, HSPA5, HYOU1, MOGS, PDIA4, QSOX2, UGGT1 |
| GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum chaperone complex | 0.000927 | DNAJC10, HSPA5, HYOU1 |

| GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum lumen | 0.00301 | DNAJC10, GANAB, HSPA5, HYOU1, PDIA4, UGGT1 |
| GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum protein-containing complex | 0.0137 | DNAJC10, GANAB, HSPA5, HYOU1 |

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING independently supports the same biological interpretation: the supplementary terms identify ER protein processing (STRING KEGG Protein processing in endoplasmic reticulum; within-query FDR = 3.07e-07), protein folding (STRING Process Protein folding; within-query FDR = 8.46e-05), ER lumen localization (STRING COMPARTMENTS Endoplasmic reticulum lumen; within-query FDR = 0.00019), ER chaperone complexes (STRING COMPARTMENTS Endoplasmic reticulum chaperone complex; within-query FDR = 0.00065) (STRING Component Endoplasmic reticulum chaperone complex; within-query FDR = 0.0014), glucosidase II (STRING COMPARTMENTS Glucosidase II complex; within-query FDR = 0.0012), and ER quality-control literature contexts (7 supplementary STRING terms; within-query FDR 0.00064–0.00082). The strongest known relationships connect DNAJC10, HSPA5, HYOU1, PDIA4, UGGT1, GANAB, and MOGS, forming a coherent ER chaperone and glycoprotein-quality-control neighborhood. hu.MAP provides partial complex support rather than a single complete module: PDIA4, GANAB, and HYOU1 recur together in (hu.MAP huMAP3_06775.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09327.1), MOGS and UGGT1 occur together in (hu.MAP huMAP3_10740.1), HSPA5 and HYOU1 occur together in (hu.MAP huMAP3_07455.1), and QSOX2 with PRKDC occurs in (hu.MAP huMAP3_09592.1). Thus, many relationships are already established at the interaction or complex level, especially for the ER core, while the complete cross-tissue co-abundance configuration is not demonstrated by these resources alone.

**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: huMAP3_06775.1 (PDIA4, GANAB, HYOU1; 3/60); huMAP3_09327.1 (PDIA4, GANAB, HYOU1; 3/93); huMAP3_10740.1 (MOGS, UGGT1; 2/3); huMAP3_07455.1 (HSPA5, HYOU1; 2/4); huMAP3_09592.1 (PRKDC, QSOX2; 2/95)

### Novelty

The molecular functions represented by the module are not novel: ER chaperoning, glycoprotein maturation, glucosidase activity, disulfide processing, and ER quality control are well established by the primary enrichment, STRING, hu.MAP, and gene annotations. The potentially new observation is the coordinated conservation of this particular gene combination and network organization across kidney and lung. The provided resources do not establish that this exact conserved co-abundance module has previously been reported, but they also cannot prove novelty merely because some edges or complexes are absent from STRING or hu.MAP. A defensible conclusion is that the module-level cross-tissue pattern may be novel or under-described, whereas its core biology is known and strongly corroborated.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the conserved module represents a shared ER proteostasis program that maintains folding and export of secretory and membrane proteins in kidney and lung cells. UGGT1 may identify incompletely folded glycoproteins for another folding cycle, MOGS and GANAB may process N-linked glycans, HSPA5 and HYOU1 may provide chaperone capacity, and DNAJC10 and PDIA4 may promote correction or disposal of misfolded proteins. QSOX2 and SCFD1 could connect oxidative folding and ER-to-Golgi trafficking to this core, while the peripheral genes may reflect cell-state or tissue-context signals. This hypothesis predicts that perturbing the core should alter ER stress, glycoprotein maturation, secretion, or ER-associated degradation in relevant kidney and lung cell types; the co-abundance data alone do not establish causality.

It participates in every primary enrichment term (6 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 5.29e-06–0.0137; see the table), has extensive STRING support across the ER chaperone and folding terms, and is represented in the hu.MAP chaperone complex (hu.MAP huMAP3_07455.1). Its extensive literature and disease-context coverage make it the clearest anchor for interpreting the module.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports HSPA5 as a central ER folding and homeostasis component of the module. It is reasonable to hypothesize that its co-abundance reflects shared demand for ER chaperone capacity in kidney and lung, but the data do not show that HSPA5 regulates the other genes.

## Module 13

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (5)**: CFAP298, EXOSC9, LAS1L, POLR2D, WDR36

### Function

The module is most consistent with a linked RNA-production and RNA-maturation program, with an additional cilia-related component. POLR2D is a core RNA polymerase II subunit and occupies the central network position. WDR36, LAS1L, and EXOSC9 have annotations involving ribosome biogenesis or RNA processing, while CFAP298 is associated with cilium assembly, cilium movement, dynein-arm assembly, and motile-cilia function. The network therefore connects general nuclear transcription with ribosome and RNA surveillance processes, while also containing a ciliary structural or functional branch. No GO/KEGG term passed global correction; consequently, the functional interpretation is based on the individual gene annotations, the network structure, and supplementary STRING evidence rather than on globally significant enrichment.

**Cell composition.** This does not appear to be a plasma-protein, secreted-protein, or blood-cell module. The HPA summary reports no secreted genes and low immune-cell specificity for every member. All genes were detected at the protein level in both kidney and lung, and most have low tissue specificity. However, the module is strongly enriched in cell-type annotations related to spermatocytes, spermatids, Sertoli cells, and ciliated cells, with POLR2D tissue-enhanced in testis. This pattern suggests a broadly expressed intracellular program that may be especially active in germ-cell or ciliated-cell contexts. It does not support a blood-contamination explanation, although shared ciliated or other specialized cell populations in kidney and lung could contribute to the conserved co-abundance signal.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 0 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

The only reported high-confidence STRING relationship is EXOSC9-WDR36 (STRING Keyword rRNA processing; within-query FDR = 0.0131), and the supplementary STRING keyword signal for rRNA processing also involves LAS1L, EXOSC9, and WDR36 (STRING Keyword rRNA processing; within-query FDR = 0.0131). POLR2D, LAS1L, and CFAP298 have no listed STRING partners in this module. No hu.MAP complex contains multiple module members. Thus, part of the RNA processing interpretation is supported by a known EXOSC9-WDR36 relationship and by the shared rRNA-processing annotation, but the complete five-gene module is not an established physical complex. The co-abundance edges involving POLR2D and CFAP298 should therefore be interpreted as coordinated abundance relationships rather than demonstrated direct molecular interactions.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 1 of 10 gene pairs: EXOSC9–WDR36 (0.77)
**STRING functional enrichment (supplementary, within-query FDR)**: Keyword rRNA processing (FDR = 0.0131, 3 genes)
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: none

### Novelty

The conserved kidney-lung co-abundance pattern is potentially a new systems-level observation because the full set is not represented as a multi-member hu.MAP complex and only one within-module STRING relationship is reported. Nevertheless, absence from STRING or hu.MAP does not establish biological novelty. Several component functions are already well established, including RNA polymerase activity, ribosome biogenesis, RNA exosome function, and motile-cilia biology. The defensible novelty claim is therefore limited to the specific cross-tissue co-abundance organization and its possible coordination of these processes, pending independent replication and functional testing.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: in a shared kidney-lung cell context, the module coordinates transcription with high RNA-processing demand and, in a subset of ciliated cells, supports production or maintenance of motile-cilia components. POLR2D may represent the transcriptional hub, WDR36 and LAS1L may reflect ribosome-biogenesis activity, EXOSC9 may provide RNA processing or surveillance capacity, and CFAP298 may mark the ciliary branch. The co-abundance relationship between the nuclear RNA-processing genes and CFAP298 could reflect coordinated cell-state regulation rather than a single physical complex. This hypothesis requires validation with cell-resolved expression, perturbation, and protein-interaction experiments.

In yeast, this polymerase subunit is associated with the polymerase under suboptimal growth conditions and may have a stress protective role. A sequence for a ribosomal pseudogene is contained within the 3' untranslated region of the transcript from this gene.…

It also has the largest literature footprint among the selected genes, supporting a relatively well-established core function.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports POLR2D as a general transcriptional machinery component and as the network's central connector. It is a plausible module organizer, but the co-abundance data do not establish that POLR2D directly regulates the other module members in kidney or lung.

Its nucleolar localization and annotations for rRNA processing and small-subunit biogenesis fit the RNA-maturation interpretation. It also has a substantial literature base and disease associations including open-angle glaucoma and respiratory disease-related phenotypes.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports WDR36 as a nucleolar RNA-biogenesis factor and as part of a known relationship with EXOSC9. Its HPA pattern includes spermatid and Sertoli-cell enhancement, which is relevant to the germ-cell signal seen across the module.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports LAS1L as a ribosome-biogenesis factor with developmental disease relevance. #### CFAP298

- **Why important**: CFAP298 is the module member most specifically tied to ciliary biology. It is connected to POLR2D and is annotated for cilium assembly, cilium movement, dynein-arm assembly, and motile-cilia function. Mutations are associated with primary ciliary dyskinesia and related phenotypes. Its HPA annotations in ciliated cell types provide a biologically meaningful explanation for why a kidney-lung module might include a ciliary component, despite its relatively limited literature volume compared with the RNA-processing genes.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports CFAP298 as a motile-cilia factor and disease-relevant primary-ciliary-dyskinesia gene. Its connection to the RNA-processing branch is a module-level observation and remains a hypothesis rather than evidence for a direct molecular interaction.

## Module 14

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (32)**: CLPB, FASTKD2, TBRG4, GNAI3, GTPBP10, ME2, MARK2, MGME1, NIPBL, PCK2, PHIP, PNPT1, PTCD3, UPF3B, RIF1, MRPL4, MRPL11, MRPL45, MRPL48, MRPL55, MRPS11, MRPS18B, MRPS22, MRPS23, MRPS27, MRPS35, SUPV3L1, DARS2, GTF3C1, TFAM, TOP2A, TOP2B

### Function

The module is most convincingly a mitochondrial gene-expression and mitochondrial translation module. Globally corrected primary evidence supports mitochondrial gene expression (GO:BP mitochondrial gene expression; q_global = 1.57e-15), organellar and mitochondrial ribosomes (GO:CC organellar ribosome; q_global = 1.8e-12)(GO:CC mitochondrial ribosome; q_global = 1.8e-12), mitochondrial translation (GO:BP mitochondrial translation; q_global = 2.91e-11), ribosome structure and translation (KEGG Ribosome - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 2.52e-12)(GO:CC ribosome; q_global = 7.48e-11)(GO:CC ribosomal subunit; q_global = 1.8e-10)(GO:MF structural constituent of ribosome; q_global = 7.27e-07)(GO:BP translation; q_global = 3.15e-05)(GO:BP protein biosynthetic process; q_global = 3.15e-05), mitochondrial RNA metabolism (GO:BP mitochondrial RNA metabolic process; q_global = 0.00143)(GO:BP mitochondrial RNA processing; q_global = 0.0124), and mitochondrial localization (GO:CC mitochondrion; q_global = 5.42e-07). The module therefore combines mitochondrial RNA handling, mitoribosome structure or assembly, and synthesis of mitochondrially encoded proteins. A smaller peripheral component involves nuclear DNA topology, chromatin, cell polarity, metabolism, and RNA surveillance. Those genes may reflect coupling to cellular growth or may be secondary co-abundance relationships rather than the core function. The evidence supports a mitochondrial translation and RNA-maintenance program, not a specific disease mechanism.

**Cell composition.** The module does not resemble a plasma-protein, secreted-protein, or blood-cell module. HPA identifies only one secreted or membrane-associated gene, while nearly all genes have low tissue specificity and low immune-cell specificity. Protein detection is broadly observed in both kidney and lung. Several genes show cell-type-enhanced expression in epithelial, neuronal, reproductive, trophoblast, and other populations, but there is no dominant blood-cell pattern. Shared abundance is therefore more consistent with a broadly expressed intracellular housekeeping or bioenergetic program across kidney and lung cells than with blood contamination. Cell-composition effects should nevertheless be checked in the original samples because some individual genes have cell-type-enhanced HPA profiles.

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING strongly supports the module's biological coherence. Dense relationships among MRPL and MRPS genes, PTCD3, and other mitochondrial RNA or translation factors agree with known mitoribosome and mitochondrial gene-expression biology (STRING Process Mitochondrial gene expression; within-query FDR = 8.9e-19)(STRING NetworkNeighborAL Organellar ribosome; within-query FDR = 4.4e-14)(STRING Process Mitochondrial translation; within-query FDR = 6.93e-14)(STRING Component Mitochondrial ribosome; within-query FDR = 7.1e-14)(STRING COMPARTMENTS Mitochondrial ribosome; within-query FDR = 7.75e-14)(STRING RCTM Mitochondrial translation initiation; within-query FDR = 1.52e-13)(STRING RCTM Mitochondrial translation elongation; within-query FDR = 1.52e-13)(STRING RCTM Mitochondrial translation termination; within-query FDR = 1.52e-13)(STRING Component Ribosome; within-query FDR = 4.8e-13). The small-subunit cluster containing MRPS11, MRPS18B, MRPS22, MRPS23, MRPS27, MRPS35, and PTCD3 is especially coherent. PNPT1 and SUPV3L1 provide a known mitochondrial RNA-processing relationship, while TOP2A and TOP2B form a separate known nuclear DNA-topology relationship. hu.MAP independently places module members in mitochondrial large- and small-subunit or mitochondrial RNA-associated complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_06589.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_05807.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_05954.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_06439.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_09113.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_07939.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_08458.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_09483.1), including the compact MRPS35–PTCD3 complex (hu.MAP huMAP3_03554.1). TOP2A, TOP2B, and PHIP occur in additional nuclear complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_07289.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_09518.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_14365.1). Thus, much of the network recapitulates known functional assemblies, although the module joins several complexes and is not one complete purified complex.

### Novelty

This should not be described as a newly discovered mitochondrial ribosome complex. The central relationships and functional theme are already represented in STRING and hu.MAP, and the constituent mitochondrial ribosomal proteins have established functions and substantial literature. The potentially novel result is the co-abundance organization of a mitochondrial translation and RNA-maintenance program across kidney and lung, together with peripheral links to metabolism, chromatin, and cell organization. Novelty therefore concerns the tissue-pair module structure and cross-tissue context, not discovery of previously unknown mitoribosome biology. Absence from STRING or hu.MAP alone does not establish novelty. Independent cohorts, orthogonal platforms, and comparison with existing kidney and lung mitochondrial signatures would be needed to support a new cross-tissue finding.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module represents a shared cellular program coordinating mitochondrial RNA maintenance, mitoribosome assembly or operation, and mitochondrial translation in kidney and lung cells. The core mitoribosomal genes may establish the co-abundance backbone, while DARS2, FASTKD2, PNPT1, SUPV3L1, and TFAM connect aminoacylation, RNA processing, RNA degradation, and mitochondrial transcription. GTPBP10 may link the core to large-subunit assembly and metabolic state, while PCK2 and ME2 may couple mitochondrial translation capacity to substrate metabolism. TOP2A, TOP2B, NIPBL, RIF1, and related genes may reflect coordination with nuclear proliferation, DNA topology, or chromatin state rather than direct membership in the mitochondrial machinery. This hypothesis predicts that greater module activity should accompany greater mitochondrial protein-synthesis capacity and respiratory demand, but the co-abundance data do not establish causality.

## Module 15

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (5)**: MCAT, MIPEP, UQCRC1, UQCRC2, TRAP1

### Function

The strongest supported interpretation is mitochondrial respiratory-chain activity, specifically ubiquinol-to-cytochrome-c electron transport and respiratory-chain complex III. This is supported by the globally corrected terms (GO:BP mitochondrial electron transport, ubiquinol to cytochrome c; q_global = 0.0132) and (GO:CC respiratory chain complex III; q_global = 0.0228), both involving UQCRC1 and UQCRC2. The broader mitochondrial interpretation is consistent with the supplementary STRING terms (STRING Keyword Transit peptide; within-query FDR = 0.0011), (STRING Keyword Mitochondrion; within-query FDR = 0.0091), (STRING RCTM Respiratory electron transport; within-query FDR = 0.0271), (STRING Component Mitochondrial respiratory chain complex III; within-query FDR = 0.0335), (STRING COMPARTMENTS Mitochondrion; within-query FDR = 0.0366), (STRING COMPARTMENTS Mitochondrial respiratory chain complex III; within-query FDR = 0.0366), (STRING NetworkNeighborAL Respiratory chain complex III; within-query FDR = 0.0419), (STRING WikiPathways Mitochondrial complex III assembly; within-query FDR = 0.0443), and (STRING Component Mitochondrion; within-query FDR = 0.0486), but those terms are not global enrichment evidence. MCAT contributes mitochondrial fatty-acid synthesis and metabolic context; MIPEP contributes maturation of mitochondrial proteins, particularly proteins related to oxidative phosphorylation; and TRAP1 contributes mitochondrial chaperoning and stress-response functions. Thus, the module most defensibly represents coordinated mitochondrial energy-production capacity, with possible support from mitochondrial protein maturation, lipid metabolism, and stress handling.

**Cell composition.** This is not a plasma-protein, secreted-protein, or blood-cell-enriched module. HPA reports no secreted genes and low immune-cell specificity for every module gene. Protein detection is present for every gene in both kidney and lung, supporting biological plausibility in both tissues. Most genes have low tissue specificity, while some show tissue enhancement in muscle or tongue and cell-type enhancement in respiratory ciliated cells, parietal cells, oocytes, or related epithelial and reproductive cell types. The shared signal could therefore reflect broadly present mitochondrial biology, with contributions from shared epithelial or ciliated cell populations, rather than contamination by circulating blood proteins.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 2 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

| Term | q_global | genes |
|---|---|---|
| GO:BP mitochondrial electron transport, ubiquinol to cytochrome c | 0.0132 | UQCRC1, UQCRC2 |
| GO:CC respiratory chain complex III | 0.0228 | UQCRC1, UQCRC2 |

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides a very strong known relationship between UQCRC1 and UQCRC2, directly supporting their participation in the same respiratory-chain complex. STRING also places the module in mitochondrial, respiratory-electron-transport, complex-III-assembly, and related functional neighborhoods through (STRING Keyword Transit peptide; within-query FDR = 0.0011), (STRING Keyword Mitochondrion; within-query FDR = 0.0091), (STRING RCTM Respiratory electron transport; within-query FDR = 0.0271), (STRING Component Mitochondrial respiratory chain complex III; within-query FDR = 0.0335), (STRING COMPARTMENTS Mitochondrion; within-query FDR = 0.0366), (STRING COMPARTMENTS Mitochondrial respiratory chain complex III; within-query FDR = 0.0366), (STRING NetworkNeighborAL Respiratory chain complex III; within-query FDR = 0.0419), (STRING WikiPathways Mitochondrial complex III assembly; within-query FDR = 0.0443), and (STRING Component Mitochondrion; within-query FDR = 0.0486). The remaining module relationships do not meet the stated STRING interaction threshold. hu.MAP independently places UQCRC1 and UQCRC2 together in each listed complex, (hu.MAP huMAP3_08546.1) through (hu.MAP huMAP3_12457.1), alongside established respiratory-chain and complex-III-associated proteins. MCAT, MIPEP, and TRAP1 have no reported hu.MAP complex or STRING partner in the supplied evidence. Therefore, the module contains a well-established complex-III core pair embedded in a co-abundance neighborhood whose other links may represent shared mitochondrial regulation, indirect coordination, or tissue-specific co-expression rather than direct physical binding.

**STRING functional enrichment (supplementary, within-query FDR)**: Keyword Transit peptide (FDR = 0.0011, 5 genes); Keyword Mitochondrion (FDR = 0.0091, 5 genes); Pfam Insulinase (Peptidase family M16) (FDR = 0.0222, 2 genes); Pfam Peptidase M16 inactive domain (FDR = 0.0222, 2 genes); RCTM Respiratory electron transport (FDR = 0.0271, 3 genes); Component Mitochondrial respiratory chain complex III (FDR = 0.0335, 2 genes); COMPARTMENTS Mitochondrion (FDR = 0.0366, 5 genes); COMPARTMENTS Mitochondrial respiratory chain complex III (FDR = 0.0366, 2 genes); KEGG Cardiac muscle contraction (FDR = 0.0404, 2 genes); KEGG Parkinson disease (FDR = 0.0404, 3 genes); NetworkNeighborAL Respiratory chain complex III (FDR = 0.0419, 2 genes); WikiPathways Mitochondrial complex III assembly (FDR = 0.0443, 2 genes); Component Mitochondrion (FDR = 0.0486, 5 genes)
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: huMAP3_08546.1 (UQCRC2, UQCRC1; 2/9); huMAP3_09600.1 (UQCRC2, UQCRC1; 2/9); huMAP3_12621.1 (UQCRC2, UQCRC1; 2/22); huMAP3_10623.1 (UQCRC2, UQCRC1; 2/24); huMAP3_12457.1 (UQCRC2, UQCRC1; 2/31)

### Novelty

The respiratory-chain interpretation itself is not a new biological discovery: UQCRC1 and UQCRC2 are already known complex-III components, are strongly connected in STRING, and recur together in hu.MAP complexes. What may be novel is the specific conserved kidney-lung co-abundance configuration that connects this known complex-III pair with MCAT, MIPEP, and TRAP1. However, the supplied evidence does not establish that this exact cross-tissue module is novel, and the lack of STRING or hu.MAP relationships for some edges cannot prove novelty. A novelty claim would require comparison with prior kidney-lung co-abundance studies, published mitochondrial modules, and independent datasets.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects coordinated regulation of mitochondrial respiratory capacity in shared kidney and lung cell populations. UQCRC1 and UQCRC2 may mark the complex-III electron-transfer core, while MIPEP may support maturation of mitochondrial oxidative-phosphorylation proteins, MCAT may connect respiratory demand with mitochondrial fatty-acid metabolism, and TRAP1 may buffer folding or oxidative stress. The observed co-abundance could therefore arise from a coordinated response that maintains mitochondrial energy production and proteostatic resilience in metabolically active epithelial or ciliated cells. This is a mechanistic hypothesis, not a demonstrated physical complex or causal pathway for every module edge.

## Module 16

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (10)**: GAR1, HOMER3, MAT2A, NOL11, NOL9, PFDN6, PSMC6, PSMD13, SNRPD1, SNRNP40

### Function

The most coherent interpretation is an intracellular RNA-processing and proteostasis module. SNRNP40 and SNRPD1 are spliceosomal small nuclear ribonucleoprotein components, while GAR1, NOL11, and NOL9 support nucleolar RNA modification, ribosome biogenesis, or ribosomal RNA maturation. PSMC6 and PSMD13 represent proteasome machinery. MAT2A supplies S-adenosylmethionine, a central methyl donor, and may connect cellular methylation capacity with RNA and protein homeostasis. The network contains a relatively strong core around SNRNP40 and a proteasome-linked pair involving PSMC6 and PSMD13, but the remaining connections are more weakly integrated. No GO or KEGG term passed global correction, so the functional interpretation rests on the concordance of gene annotations, network structure, STRING evidence, and hu.MAP evidence rather than on statistically significant GO or KEGG enrichment. STRING term S1 is supplementary and should not be treated as global enrichment.

**Cell composition.** The module is not composed of plasma or secreted proteins: the HPA summary reports no secreted genes, and the encoded proteins have intracellular nuclear, nucleolar, nucleoplasmic, cytosolic, or nuclear-speckle localizations. Most genes have low tissue specificity and low immune-cell specificity, which is more compatible with broadly used intracellular machinery than with a blood-cell or plasma-protein module. Several genes are cell-type enhanced in diverse cell types, including germline, trophoblast, epithelial, endothelial, and monocyte populations, but there is no coherent dominant blood-cell signature. HOMER3 has classical-monocyte enhancement, so a contribution from cell composition cannot be excluded, yet the overall evidence does not support interpreting this module primarily as blood contamination. Detection of many proteins in kidney and lung is consistent with a shared intracellular program, although absence of detection for some proteins in one tissue may reflect measurement limitations.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 0 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides established support for GAR1 with SNRNP40, SNRNP40 with SNRPD1, and PSMC6 with PSMD13. These relationships independently support spliceosomal and proteasomal components within the co-abundance pattern. STRING supplementary term S1 links most of its contributing genes to RNA metabolism, but it remains within-query supplementary evidence rather than globally corrected enrichment. hu.MAP independently places PSMC6 and PSMD13 together in multiple proteasome-related complexes, including a high-confidence complex, and places SNRNP40 and SNRPD1 together in a spliceosome-related complex. The fact that only a subset of module relationships is represented in STRING or hu.MAP suggests that the co-abundance module is broader than a single physical complex and may combine several coordinated cellular systems.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 3 of 45 gene pairs: GAR1–SNRNP40 (0.74); PSMC6–PSMD13 (0.999); SNRNP40–SNRPD1 (0.996)
**STRING functional enrichment (supplementary, within-query FDR)**: RCTM Metabolism of RNA (FDR = 0.0071, 7 genes)
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: huMAP3_05064.1 (PSMC6, PSMD13; 2/31); huMAP3_09656.1 (PSMC6, PSMD13; 2/64); huMAP3_13725.1 (PSMC6, PSMD13; 2/69); huMAP3_08980.1 (SNRPD1, SNRNP40; 2/97)

### Novelty

This module cannot be claimed as a wholly new molecular discovery from the fact sheet alone. Several of its relationships and functions are already established through STRING, hu.MAP, gene annotations, and extensive prior literature, especially the spliceosomal, nucleolar, and proteasomal components. Its potentially novel aspect is the conserved co-abundance coupling of these systems across kidney and lung, particularly the association with MAT2A and the weaker links among RNA processing, ribosome biogenesis, and proteostasis. However, absence of some pairs from STRING or hu.MAP does not demonstrate novelty, because database coverage, assay context, tissue composition, and detection limits can all explain missing relationships. A defensible conclusion is that the cross-tissue co-abundance organization may be a new systems-level observation, whereas the component functions and several physical interactions are established.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects a shared demand for coordinated RNA production, RNA maturation, translation-supporting nucleolar activity, and removal of damaged or misassembled proteins in kidney and lung cells. In this model, spliceosomal proteins and nucleolar factors support transcript and ribosome maturation, MAT2A supports methyl-donor production, and proteasome subunits maintain proteome quality. The observed co-abundance could therefore arise from a common cellular state involving transcriptional and translational workload or proteotoxic stress rather than from one discrete multiprotein complex. This hypothesis requires testing with independent expression datasets, cell-type-resolved measurements, perturbation experiments, and direct assays of RNA processing, methylation capacity, and proteasome activity.

## Module 17

**Tissue pair**: kidney and lung  
**Genes (9)**: MCM2, MCM4, MCM5, MCM6, MCM7, NASP, RRM1, RNASEH2B, SMC2

### Function

This is a DNA replication and chromosome-maintenance module centered on the MCM helicase machinery. The strongest primary evidence is the coherent enrichment of the MCM complex and CMG complex (GO:CC MCM complex; q_global = 4.49e-09) (GO:CC CMG complex; q_global = 2.33e-07), replication initiation and regulation of DNA replication (4 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 2.33e-07–7.54e-05; see the table), DNA helicase activities (7 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 1e-05–5.72e-05; see the table), and DNA replication itself (GO:BP DNA replication; q_global = 7.76e-06) (GO:BP DNA-templated DNA replication; q_global = 3.23e-05). The module also carries globally corrected evidence for DNA repair, homologous recombination, recombinational repair, and break-induced replication (7 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 5.61e-08–0.00552; see the table). KEGG supports DNA replication and cell-cycle involvement (KEGG DNA replication - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 3.6e-07) (KEGG Cell cycle - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 0.000401). NASP, RRM1, RNASEH2B, and SMC2 extend the core MCM signal toward chromatin handling, deoxyribonucleotide supply, RNA-DNA hybrid processing, and chromosome organization. Thus, the most defensible interpretation is a proliferative nuclear module coordinating replication-fork activity with genome maintenance, rather than a tissue-specific kidney or lung biochemical pathway.

**Cell composition.** The module is composed entirely of nuclear or cytosolic intracellular proteins, with no secreted genes in the HPA summary. HPA detects protein in kidney and lung for nearly the entire module, but the expression pattern is strongly associated with proliferative progenitor populations: erythrocyte progenitors are repeatedly enriched, and monocyte and megakaryocyte progenitors also recur. Most genes have tissue-enhanced annotation in bone marrow or lymphoid tissue, while the module has low immune-cell specificity. This combination is consistent with a cell-cycle or progenitor-abundance signal and could reflect shared proliferating cell types, including blood-cell progenitors or blood-derived material, in kidney and lung samples. It should not be interpreted as evidence that the module is secreted or uniquely characteristic of kidney or lung parenchyma. The low immune-cell specificity also argues against assigning it specifically to mature immune infiltration without additional sample-level data.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 52 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING independently recapitulates the MCM core: the MCM paralog relationships are extremely strong, and the MCM proteins connect to RRM1, NASP, and SMC2. STRING functional categories support DNA replication, the MCM and CMG complexes, single-stranded DNA binding, DNA unwinding, and break-induced replication (15 supplementary STRING terms; within-query FDR 5.67e-09–2.56e-07). These STRING terms are supplementary and do not replace the globally corrected GO/KEGG evidence. hu.MAP provides orthogonal complex-level support. The MCM genes co-occur in multiple experimentally supported complexes containing MCMBP and, in larger assemblies, MCM3, CDC45, the GINS components, TIMELESS, TIPIN, and CLSPN (hu.MAP huMAP3_05529.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_14758.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08554.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_11832.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_12180.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06061.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07113.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_11119.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09860.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_00154.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09466.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10862.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_14306.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_02602.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09238.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08848.1). NASP and MCM2 also occur together in a histone and chromatin-assembly complex (hu.MAP huMAP3_10747.1). By contrast, RRM1, SMC2, and RNASEH2B have no reported hu.MAP complex containing multiple module members, and RNASEH2B has no STRING interaction in the supplied network. The network therefore combines a well-known physical MCM core with peripheral genes linked by functional or co-abundance relationships.

### Novelty

The underlying biology is not a new molecular discovery: the MCM complex, replication initiation, genome repair, and cell-cycle functions are already well established in GO, KEGG, STRING, hu.MAP, and the gene records. The co-abundance module may still be a useful dataset-specific observation because it shows coordinated behavior across kidney and lung, but the fact sheet provides no comparison against previously reported tissue co-abundance modules or an external module catalogue. Therefore it is not justified to call the module a new biological pathway or a novel protein complex. Absence of a STRING or hu.MAP relationship for peripheral genes would not establish novelty; it more cautiously indicates that those relationships may be indirect, context-dependent, or insufficiently captured by existing interaction resources.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the shared kidney-lung module reflects the abundance of cycling progenitor-like cells or proliferating cellular compartments that require coordinated licensing and firing of replication origins, replication-fork helicase activity, chromatin handling, nucleotide production, and repair of replication-associated DNA lesions. The MCM core may form the mechanistic backbone, NASP may support histone handling during genome duplication, RRM1 may provide deoxyribonucleotides for replication and repair, SMC2 may reflect chromosome compaction during mitosis, and RNASEH2B may connect replication to processing of RNA-DNA hybrids. This is a hypothesis about the source and coordination of the tissue signal, not proof that these proteins form one physical complex in kidney or lung. It should be tested against cell-composition markers, proliferation measurements, sample blood content, and independent protein-level or single-cell data.
